\clearpage
\phantomsection\part*{Part VI.\quad A staircase of polynomial rarity}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Part VI.\texorpdfstring{\quad}{ } A staircase of polynomial rarity}

\noindent\emph{Part~VI was added on 30 September 2026 in batch~68 of
ProveIt's incoming-report intake, from one manuscript written against the
state of Parts~I--V printed above. Part~IV found the first-order profile of
$\ljP(M_n\le\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)$ for $1/2<\theta<1$; below half size it
proved only $o(n^{-1/2})$ and polynomial lower bounds
(\cref{mj:thm:profile,mj:thm:lower}). It asked for the hierarchy below half
size (\cref{mj:sec:q-hierarchy}) and for the order and the window at
$m=n/2$ (\cref{cjm:sec:mj-half}), and Part~V asked the second question again
(\cref{pr:sec:cjm-half}). Part~VI answers the exponent part of the first and
the order-level part of the second: the probability has the exact polynomial
order $n^{-\lfloor1/\theta\rfloor/2}$ for every fixed $0<\theta<1$, and the
order changes at every reciprocal threshold $1/q$ across an explicit
order-crossover scale. Its proofs use no asymptotic theorem of Parts~I--V.}

\section{Provenance, conventions and the answer}
\label{pr:sec:intro}

\subsection{One manuscript, one addition}
\label{pr:sec:source}
Part~VI is built from one AI-assisted research manuscript,
\emph{manuscript~05} of batch~68, \emph{A Staircase of Polynomial Rarity:
Bulk exponents and reciprocal-threshold crossover scales for
adjacency-bounded $132$-avoiding permutations}, dated September 30, 2026
(25 pages, US Letter, as delivered). It arrived as the archive
\texttt{ProveIt\_Polynomial\_Rarity.zip} (inner directory
\texttt{ProveIt\_Polynomial\_Rarity/}, main file \texttt{article.tex}) in
ProveIt commit \texttt{a866ff9a2}, and was placed in commit
\texttt{29e52fcdf}. It was written against ProveIt commit
\begin{center}\small\prsha,\end{center}
at which this report's \texttt{README.md}, \path{code/model.py} and
\path{code/05-macroscopic-deficits-model.py} had the blobs
\texttt{a40d20342f}, \texttt{aee5528c72} and \texttt{f0248e0d61}, the ones
the manuscript records; the manuscript did not read this report's
\texttt{article.tex}, whose blob at the pin was \texttt{0b7cb2f2e6}. All four
files were unchanged at the placement commit, so every statement the
manuscript makes about this report refers to Parts~I--V as printed above.
Its code, data, figures and source ledger are shipped beside the earlier
parts' files with the prefix \path{07-polynomial-rarity-}, continuing the
report's local sequence after Part~V's \path{06-critical-moments-}. Its
manuscript, README and PDF are not shipped and survive in the arrival
commit; its checksum ledger was verified at placement (15 of 15 files) and
retired. Its two figure PDFs were regenerated in the write phase
(\cref{pr:sec:merge}).

The manuscript contributes, in the order printed here: the complete
fixed-fraction polynomial-order staircase, reciprocal endpoints included
(\cref{pr:thm:staircase}); a uniform two-term comparison near every
reciprocal threshold (\cref{pr:thm:cross}), with its order-crossover scales
(\cref{pr:cor:scales}), in particular at half size (\cref{pr:cor:half}), and
a continuum of logarithmic exponents in shrinking neighbourhoods
(\cref{pr:cor:powerapproach}); a local theorem for truncated subcritical
renewal sequences for every power-tail index $0<\alpha<1$
(\cref{pr:thm:renewal}) with its two lemmas, and a renewal-sandwich transfer
principle (\cref{pr:prop:universality}); the two counting envelopes it
transfers through, which are Part~I's, reproved in the normalization it needs
(\cref{pr:sec:envelopes}); a discussion of robustness and limitations; exact
finite checks through $n=128$; ten research questions; a derivation of the
endpoint recurrence of its checks; and a proof audit. Every result, proof,
example, remark, limitation and question of the manuscript is printed.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Status (manuscript 05 of batch 68).} The theorems of Part~VI have
written mathematical proofs. They are not Lean- or Rocq-verified, have not
been independently refereed, and no literature priority is claimed. Unlike
Part~V, Part~VI uses no asymptotic theorem of Parts~I--V as an input: it
needs only the maximum decomposition, an explicitly verified injection
(both Part~I's, reproved here), elementary power-sum bounds and Catalan
asymptotics. Its conclusions are matching two-sided \emph{orders}, not
asymptotic equivalents: no amplitude, endpoint constant, limiting crossover
function or conditioned-permutation geometry is determined. The finite data
and the figures are regression checks and illustrations, not evidence for
the asymptotic statements. [Added 1 October 2026, batch 71: Part~VII
determines the amplitude for $1/3<\theta<1/2$, the endpoint constant
$\mathfrak K_2=6$ and the crossover function $\mathfrak H_2$ at half size
(\cref{hc:thm:window,hc:thm:phase}).] [Added 1 October 2026, batch 72: Part~VIII
determines every amplitude, endpoint constant and crossover function for fixed
indices, and the conditioned law of the parser's macroscopic pieces
(\cref{gc:thm:phase,gc:thm:window,gc:cor:geometry}).]
\end{resultbox}

\subsection{The questions of Parts~IV and~V, and what answers them}
\label{pr:sec:answer}
For fixed $1/2<\theta<1$, Part~IV proves the equivalent
\eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio},
$\ljP(M_n\le\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)\sim
\frac{3(2\theta-1)}{\sqrt{\pi\theta(1-\theta)}}\,n^{-1/2}$. For
$0<\theta\le1/2$ it proves only $o(n^{-1/2})$ (\cref{mj:thm:profile}) and
the lower bound $c\,n^{-r/2}$ whenever $(r+1)\theta>1$
(\cref{mj:thm:lower}). Its question \cref{mj:sec:q-hierarchy} asks whether,
for nonreciprocal $\theta$ and $r=\lceil1/\theta\rceil-1$, there is a positive
$\Omega_r(\theta)$ with $\ljP(M_n\le\theta n)\sim\Omega_r(\theta)n^{-r/2}$;
its question \cref{cjm:sec:mj-half} asks for the order of $\ljP(M_n\le n/2)$,
for the transition when $M_n\le n/2+s_n$, and whether the thresholds
$\theta=1/r$ mark distinct polynomial transitions. Part~V repeats the
half-size question (\cref{pr:sec:cjm-half}), and the README's list of what
Part~IV does not claim names the hierarchy and the order and window at
$m=n/2$; that list is the manuscript's starting point.

Part~VI answers these questions at the level of orders:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The exponents.} For every fixed $0<\theta<1$,
 $\ljP(M_n\le\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)=\Theta(n^{-\lfloor1/\theta\rfloor/2})$
 (\cref{pr:thm:staircase}). This answers the exponent part of
 \cref{mj:sec:q-hierarchy}; the existence and value of $\Omega_r(\theta)$
 stay open (\cref{pr:sec:q-amplitudes}). The lower bounds are Part~IV's
 \cref{mj:thm:lower}, obtained again by a different route; the matching
 upper bounds are new. For $\theta>1/2$ the theorem gives only
 $\Theta(n^{-1/2})$, which is weaker than \eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio}. [Added
 1 October 2026, batch 72: $\Omega_r(\theta)$ exists for every $r$ and is
 found in Part~VIII (\cref{gc:thm:phase}).]
\item \emph{The half-size threshold.} $\ljP(M_n\le\lfloor n/2\rfloor)=\Theta(n^{-1})$;
 the order-crossover scale is $\sqrt n$, and
 $\ljP(M_n\le\lfloor n/2+t\sqrt n\rfloor)\asymp n^{-1}(1+t_+)$ uniformly for
 bounded $t$ (\cref{pr:cor:half}). A limiting transition function is not
 proved (\cref{pr:sec:q-crossover}). [Added 1 October 2026, batch 71:
 Part~VII proves it, $6+(12/\sqrt\pi)t_+$ (\cref{hc:thm:window}).]
\item \emph{Every reciprocal threshold.} Each $\theta=1/q$, $q\ge2$, is a
 jump of the logarithmic exponent from $(q-1)/2$ to $q/2$, the endpoint
 belonging to the rarer phase, and the order changes across the scale
 $n^{1-1/(2(q-1))}$ above $n/q$ (\cref{pr:thm:cross,pr:cor:scales}). [Added
 1 October 2026, batch 72: the limiting crossover function at every $1/q$ is
 proved in Part~VIII (\cref{gc:thm:window}).]
\end{itemize}

Two indexings of the exponent meet here. Part~VI writes
$r=\lfloor1/\theta\rfloor$, Part~IV's question $r=\lceil1/\theta\rceil-1$.
They agree whenever $1/\theta$ is not an integer. At $\theta=1/q$ the first
is $q$ and the second $q-1$: Part~IV states its question for nonreciprocal
$\theta$ only, and the endpoint exponent is $q/2$, not $(q-1)/2$. In
\cref{mj:thm:lower} the condition $(r+1)\theta>1$ admits exactly the
integers $r\ge\lfloor1/\theta\rfloor$, so its best bound is the staircase
order in every case, at a reciprocal endpoint as well (as the remark after
\cref{mj:thm:lower} says).

\subsection{Independence and inherited status}
\label{pr:sec:inherited}
The manuscript read this report's README, Part~I's \path{code/model.py}
and Part~IV's \path{code/05-macroscopic-deficits-model.py}
(\cref{pr:sec:provenance}). The README's status statement calls the
earlier parts AI-assisted and unrefereed, and the manuscript therefore
avoids treating their unverified asymptotic assertions as axioms. Its proof
needs only the direct maximum decomposition, a directly verified injection,
elementary power-sum bounds, and Catalan asymptotics. It uses neither
\eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio} nor any other asymptotic theorem of Parts~I--V.
The elementary upper majorant and the skew-block lower construction are
Part~I's (\cref{prop:upper}, and \cref{prop:lower} at $d=1$); the manuscript
reproves precisely the versions it needs. Part~VI's theorems therefore do not
inherit the status of Parts~III--V, in contrast with Part~V's application.

The delivered code tests finite counting identities, not infinite
assertions. It contains no claimed Lean or Rocq formalization. Neither
finite agreement nor an attractive plot is offered as a substitute for the
proofs in \cref{pr:sec:renewal,pr:sec:application}.

\subsection{Relation to public work}
Nadler~\cite{Nadler} initiated the adjacency-constrained enumeration and
identified the restriction on the position of the maximum. Mayama and
Akita~\cite{MayamaAkita} developed the two-sided endpoint-state recurrence,
rational generating functions for fixed $m$, and the corresponding spectral
growth theory. The question of Part~VI is different: both $m$ and $n$ grow,
with $m$ a substantial fraction of $n$. The main advance is a complete
polynomial-order phase diagram, including all reciprocal boundaries, rather
than a new fixed-$m$ enumeration; a uniform two-term comparison near each
boundary explains how the exponent changes. The analytic contribution is the
local truncated-renewal comparison and its application to the two envelopes.

No claim of exhaustive literature priority is made. The mechanism belongs to
the established family of ``fewest big jumps'' ideas. Kerriou and
M\"orters~\cite{pr:KerriouMorters} prove a different, precise
large-deviation theorem for a growing number of truncated heavy-tailed
summands. The setting here is a subcritical renewal sum, with an exact
lattice target, a power-tail index below one, and estimates that remain
valid at the reciprocal threshold itself. The necessary result is proved from
first principles. The literature check (30 September 2026) used the arXiv
records of \cite{Nadler,MayamaAkita} and the record and an author-hosted PDF
of \cite{pr:KerriouMorters}, with targeted searches; it was not an exhaustive
priority search (\cref{pr:sec:provenance}). Accordingly, ``new'' in the
contribution ledger (\cref{pr:tab:ledger}) means proved here beyond the
explicit scope of Parts~I--V, not certified absent from all other
literature.

\centering\small

\label{pr:tab:ledger}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright}p{.43\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.49\linewidth}}
\toprule
In Parts~I--V & Proved in Part~VI\\
\midrule
Maximum decomposition and the Catalan majorant (Part~I, \cref{prop:upper}) & Reproved; also $\overline A_m\preceqc\Cat$\\
Injective skew blocks (Part~I, \cref{prop:lower}, $d=1$) & Reproved; both envelopes normalized as subcritical renewal kernels\\
Polynomial lower bounds below half size (Part~IV, \cref{mj:thm:lower}) & Matching upper bounds for every $0<\theta<1$, reciprocal endpoints included\\
First-order profile for $\theta>1/2$ (Part~IV, \cref{mj:thm:profile}) & Only the order $n^{-1/2}$; weaker, and not used\\
Hierarchy and half-size window asked (Parts~IV, V) & Uniform two-term comparison at every $1/q$; order-crossover scales\\
--- & Local truncated-renewal theorem for $0<\alpha<1$; a transfer principle\\
Exact endpoint recurrence (Parts~I, IV, V) & Independent finite checks through $n=128$; no formalization\\

\end{table}

\subsection{Notation of Part~VI}
\label{pr:sec:notation}
The symbols $a_n^{(m)}$, $C_n$, $\Cat$, $\Av_n(132)$, $M_n$, the generating
function $A_m(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_n^{(m)}x^n$ of Part~I, the Catalan polynomial
$K_m(x)=\sum_{k=1}^mC_{k-1}x^k$ of Part~II (with Part~I's majorant
$R_m=x+K_m$ of \eqref{eq:R}), Part~I's block polynomial $K_{m,1}$
\eqref{eq:K1}, Part~IV's fixed fraction $\theta$ and conjectured amplitude
$\Omega_r(\theta)$, and the endpoint counts $T^{(m)}_{u,v}(n)$,
$\mathcal N_n(u)$, $c_{k,u}$ and $N_{n,f}$ of Parts~III--V keep their
meanings. Several of the manuscript's letters are used in Parts~I--V for
other objects. Part~VI fixes one name for each notion
(\cref{pr:tab:notation}). In particular:
\begin{itemize}
\item $p_n(m)=\ljP(M_n\le m)$ is a probability; it is not a moment exponent
 $p$ as in Parts~III--V, which occurs in Part~VI only in the moment bound
 \eqref{pr:eq:momentbound};
\item the two envelopes are $\underline A_m\preceqc A_m\preceqc\overline A_m$
 (the manuscript's $L_m$ and $B_m$): $\overline A_m=1+x/(1-R_m)$ is Part~I's
 majorant \eqref{eq:upper}, and $\underline A_m=(1-K_{m,1})^{-1}$ is
 Part~I's block lower bound \eqref{eq:root-sandwich} at $d=1$; the
 manuscript's $H_{m-1}(x)$ is Part~I's $K_{m,1}(x)$. The tempting false
 reading of the manuscript's $B_1$ is Part~V's endpoint constant $B_1$ or
 Part~I's mass $B_d$ at $d=1$;
\item the ratio interval is $\theta_-\le m/n\le\theta_+$ (the manuscript's
 $a\le m/n\le b$), since $a_n^{(m)}$ counts avoiders and $a$, $b$ are block
 sizes in Parts~IV and~V;
\item the order function is $\mathcal O_q(n,m)$ (the manuscript's $F_q$),
 since $F^{(m)}_{p,q}$ are Part~I's endpoint series; the order-crossover
 scale is $\mathfrak w_q(n)$ (the manuscript's $w_q$), since $w_k$ are
 renewal weights;
\item the renewal kernels and weights $W$, $w_k$ keep the manuscript's
 letters; their total masses are $\bar w$, $\bar w_\pm$ (the manuscript's
 $\rho$, $\rho_\pm$), since $\rho=-\Psi'$ is Part~IV's deficit density; the
 resolvents are $Z$ (the manuscript's $R$), since $R_m$ is Part~I's
 majorant polynomial;
\item the conjectural endpoint constants, slopes and limiting crossover
 functions are $\mathfrak K_q$, $\mathfrak L_q$, $\mathfrak H_q(t)$ (the
 manuscript's $K_q$, $L_q$, $H_q$), since $K_m$, $K_{m,d}$, $L_m$ and $H$
 are polynomials, a logarithm and a series of Parts~I--III.
\end{itemize}

$\theta_-$, $\theta_+$ & ends of a fixed ratio interval & $a$, $b$ & the counts
 $a_n^{(m)}$; block sizes $a,b$.\\
$\mathcal O_q(n,m)$ & order function of \cref{pr:thm:cross} & $F_q(n,m)$ &
 Part~I's series $F^{(m)}_{p,q}$; Part~III's first entry $F$.\\
$\mathfrak w_q(n)$ & order-crossover scale $n^{1-1/(2(q-1))}$ & $w_q(n)$ &
 the renewal weights $w_k$.\\
$\overline A_m$, $\underline A_m$ & upper and lower envelopes of $A_m$ &
 $B_m$, $L_m$ & Part~V's $B_0,B_1$; Part~I's $B_d$, $L_m$.\\
$K_{m,1}$ & lower block polynomial & $H_{m-1}$ & Part~III's series $H$;
 Part~V's $\mathcal H$.\\
$\mathfrak B_k$ & set of lower blocks of size $k$ & $\mathcal B_k$ &
 Part~II's $\Bformal_N$.\\
$w_k^\pm$, $W^\pm_M$; $w_k$, $W_m$ & renewal weights and kernels & same &
 Part~I's endpoint matrix $W_m(x)$; Part~I's weights $b_k$.\\
$\bar w_\pm$, $\bar w$ & total kernel masses & $\rho_\pm$, $\rho$ &
 Part~IV's density $\rho=-\Psi'$.\\
$Z^\pm_M$, $Z_m$, $Z$ & renewal resolvents $(1-W)^{-1}$ & $R^\pm_M$, $R_m$,
 $R$ & Part~I's majorant $R_m=x+K_m$; Part~IV's $Z_n(f)$.\\
$u_M^\pm(n)$, $u_m(n)$ & renewal coefficients & same & Part~IV's
 threshold fraction $u$.\\
$\alpha$ & power-tail index, $0<\alpha<1$ & $\alpha$ & Part~I's growth
 constants $\alpha_m$ (it is Part~V's abstract tail exponent).\\
$\eta_q(\beta)$, $\beta$ & exponent of \cref{pr:cor:powerapproach} &
 $\eta_q(\beta)$, $\beta$ & Part~V's measure $\eta_n$; Part~II's
 $\beta_m$.\\
$\lambda_\star$, $\tilde a^{(m)}_n$ & growth base and counts of
 \cref{pr:prop:universality} & $\Lambda$, $b^{(m)}_n$ & Part~II's
 $\Lambda=\frac12\log m$; Part~I's $b_k$.\\
$\mathfrak K_q$, $\mathfrak L_q$, $\mathfrak H_q(t)$ & conjectural endpoint
 constant, slope, crossover function & $K_q$, $L_q$, $H_q$ & Part~II's $K_m$;
 Part~I's $K_{m,d}$, $L_m$; Part~III's $H$; Part~IV's $\mathfrak k$.\\
$N_{n,n-d}$, $\mathcal N_n(u)$, $T^{\mathrm{free}}_{u,v}(n)$,
 $T^{(m)}_{u,v}(n)$, $c_{k,u}$ & the counts of \cref{pr:app:dp} &
 $f(n,d)$, $h(n,u)$, $T_{\mathrm{free}}$, $T$, $c(k,u)$ & Parts~IV and~V's
 names for the same counts.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

No normalization changed: every renamed symbol denotes exactly the
manuscript's quantity. Probability is written $\ljP$ as in Parts~III--V,
the Catalan generating function $\Cat$ as in Part~I. Throughout Part~VI,
\[
 f_n\asymp g_n
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 0<c\le f_n/g_n\le C<\infty
 \quad\hbox{for all sufficiently large }n,
\]
and $\Theta$ has the same meaning. The constants may depend on fixed
parameters stated in the theorem, but not on varying $n$ or $m$. This
notation does \emph{not} mean that the ratio converges; in particular, the
scale functions printed here are not numerical approximations to
probabilities. The letters $S$, $T$, $V$, $J$, $L$, $A$, $h$, $\ell$,
$\gamma$, $\delta$, $\varepsilon$, $\lambda_n$, $\Delta$, $d_i$, $r_s$, $s$,
$e$, $f$, $c_w$, $C_w$ and $k_0$ are local to \cref{pr:sec:renewal} and to
single proofs, and are declared where used; in particular $S_n(z)$,
$T_n(z)$ and $V_{n,m}(z)$ are not Part~V's survival functions, Part~III's
cost $T$ or Part~I's component $V_m$. Equations, tables and figures are
numbered consecutively with those of Parts~I--V.

\section{Main results}
\label{pr:sec:main}

\subsection{Objects and probabilities}
A permutation $\pi=(\pi_1,\ldots,\pi_n)$ avoids $132$ when no indices
$i<j<k$ satisfy $\pi_i<\pi_k<\pi_j$. Write $\Av_n(132)$ for this class and
\begin{equation}
 C_n=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n},\qquad
 M(\pi)=\max_{1\le i<n}|\pi_{i+1}-\pi_i|,
 \qquad
 a_n^{(m)}=\#\{\pi\in\Av_n(132):M(\pi)\le m\}.
 \label{pr:eq:objects}
\end{equation}
For $n\le1$ the maximum of the empty set of jumps is defined to be zero. A
uniformly chosen avoider has largest jump $M_n$, so
\begin{equation}
 p_n(m):=\ljP(M_n\le m)=\frac{a_n^{(m)}}{C_n}.
 \label{pr:eq:prob}
\end{equation}
The Catalan enumeration is recalled in \cref{pr:sec:envelopes}.

\subsection{The staircase and the reciprocal thresholds}
\begin{theorem}[Complete polynomial-order staircase]\label{pr:thm:staircase}
For every fixed real $0<\theta<1$, put $r=\lfloor1/\theta\rfloor$. Then
\begin{equation}
 p_n(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)=\Theta_\theta(n^{-r/2}),
 \qquad
 a_n^{(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)}
 =\Theta_\theta\!\left(4^n n^{-(r+3)/2}\right).
 \label{pr:eq:staircase}
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{-\log p_n(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)}{\log n}
 =\frac12\left\lfloor\frac1\theta\right\rfloor.
 \label{pr:eq:lograte}
\end{equation}
The constants are uniform on compact subintervals of each open phase. At
every reciprocal endpoint $\theta=1/q$, $q\ge2$, the exponent is $q/2$, not
$(q-1)/2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform reciprocal-threshold comparison]\label{pr:thm:cross}
Fix an integer $q\ge2$ and real constants $\theta_-,\theta_+$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{q+1}<\theta_-<\frac1q<\theta_+<\frac1{q-1}.
 \label{pr:eq:ab}
\end{equation}
There exist $c,C>0$ and $N$ such that for every integer $n\ge N$ and every
integer $m$ with $\theta_-\le m/n\le\theta_+$,
\begin{equation}
 c\,\mathcal O_q(n,m)\le p_n(m)\le C\,\mathcal O_q(n,m),
 \qquad
 \mathcal O_q(n,m):=n^{-q/2}
       +n^{-3(q-1)/2}\prpos{qm-n}^{q-1}.
 \label{pr:eq:cross}
\end{equation}
The constants depend only on $q,\theta_-,\theta_+$.
\end{theorem}

The first term is the rarity of a configuration with $q+1$ macroscopic
pieces. The second is the contribution that becomes available when $q$
almost-maximal pieces can accommodate the total size. This explanation
concerns the counting envelopes; it is not yet a theorem identifying the
typical pieces of the conditioned permutation.

\begin{corollary}[The order-crossover scales]\label{pr:cor:scales}
Define
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak w_q(n):=n^{1-1/(2(q-1))}.
 \label{pr:eq:window}
\end{equation}
Under the assumptions of \cref{pr:thm:cross},
\begin{equation}
 p_n(m)\asymp n^{-q/2}
 \left[1+\left(\frac{\prpos{qm-n}}{\mathfrak w_q(n)}\right)^{q-1}\right].
 \label{pr:eq:crossfactored}
\end{equation}
In particular, $m=\lfloor n/q\rfloor+O(\mathfrak w_q(n))$ gives
$p_n(m)=\Theta(n^{-q/2})$. If $s_n=m-n/q>0$,
$s_n/\mathfrak w_q(n)\to\infty$, and $s_n=o(n)$, then
\begin{equation}
 p_n(m)\asymp n^{-3(q-1)/2}s_n^{q-1}.
 \label{pr:eq:outsidewindow}
\end{equation}
On the lower side of the reciprocal threshold, throughout any fixed
neighbourhood allowed by \eqref{pr:eq:ab}, the order remains $n^{-q/2}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[The first unresolved threshold]\label{pr:cor:half}
Uniformly for $m/n$ in a fixed sufficiently small neighbourhood of $1/2$,
\begin{equation}
 p_n(m)\asymp \frac1n+\frac{\prpos{2m-n}}{n^{3/2}}.
 \label{pr:eq:half}
\end{equation}
Thus
\[
 p_n(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)=\Theta(n^{-1}),\qquad
 p_n(\lfloor n/2+t\sqrt n\rfloor)\asymp n^{-1}(1+t_+)
\]
uniformly for $t$ in any fixed bounded interval. There is no assertion here
that $n\,p_n(\lfloor n/2+t\sqrt n\rfloor)$ has a limit.
\end{corollary}

[Added 1 October 2026, batch 71: it has one. Part~VII proves
$n\,p_n(\lfloor n/2+t\sqrt n\rfloor)\to6+(12/\sqrt\pi)t_+$ uniformly for
bounded $t$ (\cref{hc:thm:window}).]

(The title is the manuscript's: $m=n/2$ is the first threshold that
Parts~IV and~V left unresolved.)

\section{Two elementary counting envelopes}
\label{pr:sec:envelopes}
This section reproves, in the form Part~VI needs, Part~I's scalar majorant
(\cref{prop:upper}) and the $d=1$ case of its injective block lower bound
(\cref{prop:lower}); the manuscript's proofs follow Part~I's arguments and
are kept because its normalization and checks are built on them.

\subsection{Maximum decomposition and Catalan enumeration}
If the maximum $n$ occurs at position $k$ in a $132$-avoiding permutation,
then every entry to its left is larger than every entry to its right.
Indeed, a left entry smaller than a right entry, together with $n$, would be
a $132$ pattern. Conversely, choosing any avoiding left block and any
avoiding right block, with all left values above all right values, produces
an avoider (Part~I's maximum decomposition, \cref{sec:endpoints}). Hence
\begin{equation}
 C_0=1,\qquad C_n=\sum_{k=1}^n C_{k-1}C_{n-k}.
 \label{pr:eq:catalanrec}
\end{equation}
The generating function $\Cat(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}C_nx^n$ therefore satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \Cat(x)=1+x\Cat(x)^2
 =\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4x}}{2x}.
 \label{pr:eq:catgf}
\end{equation}
The binomial expansion gives the formula in \eqref{pr:eq:objects}.
Stirling's formula gives
\begin{equation}
 C_n\sim\frac{4^n}{\sqrt\pi\,n^{3/2}},
 \qquad
 c_{\rm Cat}(n+1)^{-3/2}\le4^{-n}C_n\le C_{\rm Cat}(n+1)^{-3/2}
 \label{pr:eq:catasymp}
\end{equation}
for some absolute positive constants $c_{\rm Cat},C_{\rm Cat}$ and all
$n\ge0$.

If $k<n$, the right block has size $n-k$ and values $1,\ldots,n-k$. The jump
immediately after the maximum is at least $k$. Thus an adjacency-bounded
avoider has its maximum either at the end or in one of the first $m$
positions. This is the structural observation of Nadler~\cite{Nadler},
reproduced here because it is the only input needed for the upper envelope.

\subsection{The valid scalar upper majorant}
With $K_m(x)=\sum_{k=1}^mC_{k-1}x^k$, let
\begin{equation}
 \overline A_m(x):=1+\frac{x}{1-x-K_m(x)}=1+\frac{x}{1-R_m(x)},
 \label{pr:eq:KandB}
\end{equation}
where $R_m=x+K_m$ is Part~I's \eqref{eq:R}. For formal series, $F\preceqc G$
means coefficientwise inequality.

\begin{lemma}[Upper envelope]\label{pr:lem:upper}
For every $m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 A_m(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_n^{(m)}x^n\preceqc\overline A_m(x).
 \label{pr:eq:upper}
\end{equation}
Moreover $\overline A_m\preceqc\Cat$.
\end{lemma}
The first assertion is Part~I's \eqref{eq:upper}.
\begin{proof}[The manuscript's proof]
For $n\ge2$, deleting a final maximum leaves an adjacency-bounded avoider of
size $n-1$. If the maximum is at position $k<n$, its left block can be chosen
in at most $C_{k-1}$ ways and its right block in at most $a_{n-k}^{(m)}$
ways. We ignore the endpoint compatibility conditions, which can only
increase the count. Consequently
\begin{equation}
 a_n^{(m)}\le a_{n-1}^{(m)}+
 \sum_{k=1}^{\min(m,n-1)}C_{k-1}a_{n-k}^{(m)}.
 \label{pr:eq:upperrec}
\end{equation}
The sequence with initial values $b_0=b_1=1$ and equality in this
recurrence has generating function $\overline A_m$. Induction proves the
first assertion.

For the second assertion, increase $m$ to infinity coefficientwise. Since
$K_\infty=x\Cat(x)$, the identity $\Cat=1+x\Cat^2$ implies
\[
 \frac1{1-x-x\Cat(x)}=\Cat(x)^2,
 \qquad \overline A_\infty(x)=1+x\Cat(x)^2=\Cat(x).
\]
All intermediate series have nonnegative coefficients.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A tempting but invalid shortcut]\label{pr:rem:falsemajorant}
The upper bound is \emph{not} $A_m\preceqc(1-K_m)^{-1}$. At $m=2$, the latter
series has coefficient $3$ at $x^3$, whereas $a_3^{(2)}=C_3=5$. The extra $x$
in the denominator of \eqref{pr:eq:KandB} accounts for a final maximum.
Part~II explicitly warns against confusing its stronger spectral comparison
with a coefficientwise upper bound (\cref{crem:rem:not-quotient}, with the
same counterexample in \cref{crem:sec:m2}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{An injective skew-block lower envelope}
For a permutation $\sigma$ of size $u$ and $\tau$ of size $v$, their skew sum
is
\[
 \sigma\ominus\tau=(\sigma_1+v,\ldots,\sigma_u+v,\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_v).
\]
Skew sums preserve $132$-avoidance: a triple spanning distinct blocks cannot
have its first entry smaller than a later entry from a lower-valued block.

For each $k\ge1$, use the following blocks:
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak B_1=\{(1)\},\qquad
 \mathfrak B_k=\{(k-1,\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_{k-2},k):
               \sigma\in\Av_{k-2}(132)\}\quad(k\ge2).
 \label{pr:eq:blocks}
\end{equation}
Every such block avoids $132$, starts either at its maximum ($k=1$) or one
below its maximum ($k\ge2$), and ends at its maximum. There are $C_{k-2}$
blocks of size $k\ge2$. These are Part~I's admissible blocks with $d=1$.

\begin{lemma}[Lower envelope]\label{pr:lem:lower}
For $m\ge2$, define
\begin{equation}
 K_{m,1}(x)=x+\sum_{k=2}^{m-1}C_{k-2}x^k,
 \qquad
 \underline A_m(x):=\frac1{1-K_{m,1}(x)}.
 \label{pr:eq:lowergf}
\end{equation}
Then $\underline A_m\preceqc A_m$.
\end{lemma}
This is Part~I's \eqref{eq:root-sandwich} at $d=1$, with $K_{m,1}$ as in
\eqref{eq:K1}.
\begin{proof}[The manuscript's proof]
Take any finite ordered list of blocks from \eqref{pr:eq:blocks}, each of
size at most $m-1$, and form their skew sum. A jump within a block is at
most its size minus one. If a block of size $k$ is followed by a block whose
first deficiency is $d\in\{0,1\}$, the jump across their boundary is
$k+d\le m$. Thus the resulting permutation has all jumps at most $m$ and
avoids $132$.

It remains to prove injectivity. A nonempty block ending at its maximum
cannot be split as a nontrivial skew sum, because in such a split every value
in the first part would have to exceed the final maximum. Each block is
therefore skew-indecomposable. Every permutation has a unique decomposition
into skew-indecomposable blocks: the possible cut positions are exactly those
$t$ for which all first $t$ values exceed all later values, and cutting at
all these positions gives the unique decomposition. Our block list is
recovered by those cuts, and each internal $\sigma$ is recovered by deleting
the first and last entries. The sequence construction has generating
function $\underline A_m$.
\end{proof}

This is precisely the simplest, first-deficiency-one specialization of
Part~I's skew-block lower construction (\cref{prop:lower}; the function
\texttt{block\_counts} of \path{code/model.py}). Its role here is important:
one need not approximate the whole class accurately in order to recover its
polynomial exponents. A small, explicit subclass suffices when it has the
correct power tail.

\subsection{Normalizing at the Catalan radius}
Replace $x$ by $z/4$. The upper renewal kernel is
\begin{equation}
 W_m^+(z)=\sum_{k=1}^m w_k^+z^k,
 \qquad
 w_1^+=\frac12,\qquad
 w_k^+=4^{-k}C_{k-1}\quad(k\ge2).
 \label{pr:eq:weightsplus}
\end{equation}
The doubled coefficient at $k=1$ is the sum of the append term and the $k=1$
term of $K_m$, as in Part~I's $R_m$, whose coefficient of $x$ is two
(\eqref{eq:R}).
Its total mass is
\begin{equation}
 \bar w_+:=\sum_{k\ge1}w_k^+=\frac14+\frac14\Cat(1/4)=\frac34.
 \label{pr:eq:massplus}
\end{equation}
The lower kernel is
\begin{equation}
 W_{m-1}^-(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}w_k^-z^k,
 \qquad w_1^-=\frac14,\qquad
 w_k^-=4^{-k}C_{k-2}\quad(k\ge2),
 \label{pr:eq:weightsminus}
\end{equation}
with total mass
\begin{equation}
 \bar w_-:=\sum_{k\ge1}w_k^-=\frac14+\frac1{16}\Cat(1/4)=\frac38,
 \label{pr:eq:massminus}
\end{equation}
which is Part~I's $B_d$ \eqref{eq:Bd} at $d=1$. Both kernels are strictly
subcritical and have power tails
\begin{equation}
 w_k^+\sim\frac{1}{4\sqrt\pi}k^{-3/2},\qquad
 w_k^-\sim\frac{1}{16\sqrt\pi}k^{-3/2}
 \label{pr:eq:twotails}
\end{equation}
(the second is Part~I's \eqref{eq:cd-tail} at $d=1$). Write
\[
 Z_M^\pm(z)=\frac1{1-W_M^\pm(z)},\qquad
 u_M^\pm(n)=\prcoef zn Z_M^\pm(z).
\]
For $n\ge1$, the exact coefficientwise sandwich is
\begin{equation}
 u_{m-1}^-(n)\le4^{-n}a_n^{(m)}
 \le\frac14u_m^+(n-1).
 \label{pr:eq:normalizedsandwich}
\end{equation}
The finite shifts $m-1$ and $n-1$ must be retained until the final
comparison. Dropping them before checking the reciprocal boundary would
obscure the endpoint issue.

\section{A local theorem for truncated subcritical renewals}
\label{pr:sec:renewal}

\subsection{Abstract hypotheses and theorem}
Let $0<\alpha<1$ and let $(w_k)_{k\ge1}$ be a nonnegative sequence
satisfying
\begin{equation}
 \bar w:=\sum_{k\ge1}w_k<1,\qquad
 w_k\le C_w k^{-1-\alpha}\ (k\ge1),\qquad
 w_k\ge c_w k^{-1-\alpha}\ (k\ge k_0),
 \label{pr:eq:generalassump}
\end{equation}
where $C_w,c_w>0$ and $k_0\ge1$ are fixed. Put
\begin{equation}
 W_m(z)=\sum_{k=1}^m w_kz^k,\quad
 Z_m(z)=\frac1{1-W_m(z)},\quad
 u_m(n)=\prcoef zn Z_m(z).
 \label{pr:eq:renewaldef}
\end{equation}
These coefficients count weighted ordered compositions whose parts are at
most $m$. One can also view them as a geometric-sum model, with an
appropriate normalizing factor; no probabilistic normalization is needed
below.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform local truncated-renewal estimate]\label{pr:thm:renewal}
Assume \eqref{pr:eq:generalassump}. Fix $q\ge2$ and
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{q+1}<\theta_-<\theta_+<\frac1{q-1}.
 \label{pr:eq:abrenewal}
\end{equation}
The interval need not straddle $1/q$. Uniformly for integer $m,n$ with $n$
sufficiently large and $\theta_-\le m/n\le\theta_+$,
\begin{equation}
 u_m(n)\asymp
 n^{-1-(q+1)\alpha}
 +n^{-q(1+\alpha)}\prpos{qm-n}^{q-1}.
 \label{pr:eq:renewalmain}
\end{equation}
The constants depend only on $\alpha$, the kernel in
\eqref{pr:eq:generalassump}, and $q,\theta_-,\theta_+$.
\end{theorem}

The theorem uses only upper and lower power bounds, not a precise tail
equivalent or regular variation with a limiting constant. It therefore
determines orders robustly, but cannot by itself identify exact amplitudes.

\subsection{A uniform local tail bound for unrestricted renewals}
Let $W(z)=\sum_{k\ge1}w_kz^k$ and $Z(z)=(1-W(z))^{-1}$.

\begin{lemma}[Local subcritical convolution bound]\label{pr:lem:local}
For every fixed integer $h\ge1$, there is $C_h<\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \prcoef zs Z(z)^h\le C_h(s+1)^{-1-\alpha}\qquad(s\ge0).
 \label{pr:eq:localtail}
\end{equation}
The same bound holds for $(1-S(z))^{-h}$ whenever $S$ is a coefficientwise
subseries of $W$. Also
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{s=0}^n s^p\prcoef zs(1-S(z))^{-h}
 \le C_{h,p}n^{p-\alpha}\qquad(p>\alpha,
 \ n\ge1).
 \label{pr:eq:momentbound}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $s\ge1$ and $\ell\ge1$, every ordered composition $k_1+\cdots+k_\ell=s$
has an index $i$ with $k_i\ge s/\ell$. Designate one such index; allowing all
possibilities only overcounts. Once the other $\ell-1$ parts are fixed, $k_i$
is determined. Its weight is at most $C_w\ell^{1+\alpha}s^{-1-\alpha}$.
Summing the other weights without the sum constraint gives at most
$\bar w^{\ell-1}$. Hence
\begin{equation}
 \prcoef zs W(z)^\ell
 \le C_w\ell^{2+\alpha}\bar w^{\ell-1}s^{-1-\alpha}.
 \label{pr:eq:ellbound}
\end{equation}
Now expand
\[
 Z(z)^h=\sum_{\ell\ge0}\binom{\ell+h-1}{h-1}W(z)^\ell.
\]
The sum of the bounds \eqref{pr:eq:ellbound} converges, because $\bar w<1$
and the remaining factors grow polynomially in $\ell$. The coefficient at
$s=0$ is one. This proves \eqref{pr:eq:localtail}. Coefficientwise
domination proves the assertion for $S$. Finally, sum $s^{p-1-\alpha}$ over
$1\le s\le n$; this is $O(n^{p-\alpha})$ because $p>\alpha$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Suppressing a macroscopic sum made only of small parts}
For a fixed small $\varepsilon>0$, put
\begin{equation}
 S_n(z)=\sum_{k\le\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor}w_kz^k,
 \qquad T_n(z)=(1-S_n(z))^{-1}.
 \label{pr:eq:smallkernel}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Arbitrarily strong polynomial suppression]\label{pr:lem:small}
Given $c>0$, $A>0$ and a fixed integer $h\ge1$, one can choose
$\varepsilon>0$ so that, for all sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation}
 \prcoef zs T_n(z)^h\le C n^{-A}\qquad(s\ge cn).
 \label{pr:eq:smallsuppress}
\end{equation}
The same $\varepsilon$ works for any prescribed finite set of values of
$h$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose $L>A/c$ and then $\varepsilon>0$ so small that $L\varepsilon<\alpha$.
Set $\lambda_n=L\log n/n$. Since $e^t-1\le te^t$ for $t\ge0$,
\begin{align*}
 S_n(e^{\lambda_n})-S_n(1)
 &\le\lambda_n e^{\lambda_n\varepsilon n}
        \sum_{k\le\varepsilon n}k w_k\\
 &\le C\frac{\log n}{n}\,n^{L\varepsilon}
                  n^{1-\alpha}
 =O\!\left((\log n)n^{L\varepsilon-\alpha}\right)=o(1).
\end{align*}
Here $\sum_{k\le N}k w_k=O(N^{1-\alpha})$ follows directly from
\eqref{pr:eq:generalassump}. Because $S_n(1)\le\bar w<1$, for large $n$ we
have
\[
 T_n(e^{\lambda_n})\le\frac{2}{1-\bar w}.
\]
All coefficients are nonnegative, so
\[
 \prcoef zs T_n(z)^h
 \le e^{-\lambda_ns}T_n(e^{\lambda_n})^h
 \le \left(\frac{2}{1-\bar w}\right)^h n^{-Lc}
 \le Cn^{-A}
\]
for $s\ge cn$. Only the final constant depends on $h$.
\end{proof}

The cutoff is a fixed fraction $\varepsilon n$, not $n/\log n$. That
distinction prevents artificial logarithmic losses. The parameter
$\varepsilon$ can depend on the desired power $A$, but remains fixed while
$n$ tends to infinity.

\subsection{Proof of the upper estimate}
\begin{proof}[Upper bound in \cref{pr:thm:renewal}]
Set $\gamma=1-(q-1)\theta_+>0$. Choose $A>1+(q+1)\alpha$. Apply
\cref{pr:lem:small} with $c=\gamma$ and choose $\varepsilon<\theta_-/2$
sufficiently small for the resulting estimate. Decompose
\[
 W_m=S_n+V_{n,m},\qquad
 V_{n,m}(z)=\sum_{\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor<k\le m}w_kz^k.
\]
Abbreviate these series by $S,V$ and put $T=(1-S)^{-1}$. The exact formal
identity
\begin{equation}
 Z_m(z)=\sum_{j\ge0}V(z)^jT(z)^{j+1}
 \label{pr:eq:markedexpansion}
\end{equation}
marks the $j$ large parts and the $j+1$ intervening sequences of small
parts. Because every part counted by $V$ exceeds $\varepsilon n$, only
$j\le J:=\lceil1/\varepsilon\rceil$ can contribute to the coefficient of
$z^n$. Thus the number of cases is fixed independently of $n$.

\paragraph{Fewer than $q$ large parts.}
For $j\le q-1$, the total size supplied by the large parts is at most
$jm\le(q-1)\theta_+n$. The small-part sequences must therefore supply at
least $\gamma n$. By \cref{pr:lem:small}, every relevant coefficient of
$T^{j+1}$ is $O(n^{-A})$. Convolving with $V^j$ and using
$V(1)^j\le\bar w^j$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \prcoef zn V^jT^{j+1}=O(n^{-A})
 =O(n^{-1-(q+1)\alpha}).
 \label{pr:eq:fewlarge}
\end{equation}
This argument includes $j=0$.

\paragraph{At least $q+1$ large parts.}
For each fixed $j\ge q+1$, an individual large-part weight is
$O(n^{-1-\alpha})$. For a specified sum $n-s$, the number of ordered
$j$-tuples is $O(n^{j-1})$. Hence
\[
 \prcoef z{n-s} V(z)^j=O(n^{-1-j\alpha})
\]
uniformly in $s$. Since $T(1)^{j+1}\le(1-\bar w)^{-j-1}$,
\begin{equation}
 \prcoef zn V^jT^{j+1}=O(n^{-1-j\alpha})
 =O(n^{-1-(q+1)\alpha}).
 \label{pr:eq:manylarge}
\end{equation}
There are at most $J$ such terms.

\paragraph{Exactly $q$ large parts.}
Put $\Delta=qm-n$. If the small-part sequences have total size $s$, write the
$q$ large parts as $k_i=m-d_i$. Their deficits satisfy
\[
 d_i\ge0,\qquad d_1+\cdots+d_q=\Delta+s.
\]
Ignoring upper bounds on the $d_i$ gives at most
\[
 \binom{\Delta+s+q-1}{q-1}
 \le C(\Delta_++s+1)^{q-1}
\]
choices when $\Delta+s\ge0$, and no choices otherwise. Every large-part
product is $O(n^{-q(1+\alpha)})$. Writing $r_s=\prcoef zs T^{q+1}$, we obtain
\begin{align}
 \prcoef zn V^qT^{q+1}
 &\le Cn^{-q(1+\alpha)}
       \sum_{s=0}^n(\Delta_++s+1)^{q-1}r_s\notag\\
 &\le Cn^{-q(1+\alpha)}
       \left((\Delta_++1)^{q-1}+n^{q-1-\alpha}\right).
 \label{pr:eq:criticalupper}
\end{align}
The last step uses $\sum r_s\le(1-\bar w)^{-q-1}$, the elementary bound
$(x+y)^{q-1}\le C_q(x^{q-1}+y^{q-1})$, and \cref{pr:lem:local} with
$p=q-1>\alpha$.

The second term in \eqref{pr:eq:criticalupper} is exactly of order
$n^{-1-(q+1)\alpha}$. The harmless constant in $(\Delta_++1)^{q-1}$ is
absorbed by that term because
\[
 q(1+\alpha)-\bigl(1+(q+1)\alpha\bigr)=q-1-\alpha>0.
\]
Combining the three cases proves the upper half of \eqref{pr:eq:renewalmain}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of the lower estimate}
\begin{proof}[Lower bound in \cref{pr:thm:renewal}]
All terms of $Z_m=\sum_{j\ge0}W_m^j$ are nonnegative, so it suffices to
exhibit two families of compositions.

\paragraph{The background family with $q+1$ macroscopic parts.}
Because $\theta_->1/(q+1)$, choose a fixed $\delta>0$ so small that
\[
 \frac1{q+1}-q\delta>0,
 \qquad \frac1{q+1}+q\delta<\theta_-.
\]
Choose the first $q$ parts independently in integer intervals of width
proportional to $n$ about $n/(q+1)$, with deviation at most $\delta n$. Let
the final part make the sum equal to $n$. Every part then lies between fixed
positive multiples of $n$ and is at most $m$. There are at least $c n^q$
choices for sufficiently large $n$. The lower tail bound in
\eqref{pr:eq:generalassump} gives a weight of at least
$c n^{-(q+1)(1+\alpha)}$ for each choice. Consequently
\begin{equation}
 u_m(n)\ge c n^{-1-(q+1)\alpha}.
 \label{pr:eq:backgroundlower}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{The near-cap family with $q$ macroscopic parts.}
Suppose $\Delta=qm-n\ge0$. Every weak composition
\[
 d_1+\cdots+d_q=\Delta,\qquad d_i\ge0
\]
gives $k_i=m-d_i$ with total size $n$. Moreover
\[
 k_i\ge m-\Delta=n-(q-1)m\ge\gamma n>0,
 \qquad k_i\le m.
\]
Thus all these choices are admissible, all parts are macroscopic, and there
are exactly $\binom{\Delta+q-1}{q-1}$ of them. Each weight is at least
$c n^{-q(1+\alpha)}$, so
\begin{equation}
 u_m(n)\ge c n^{-q(1+\alpha)}(\Delta+1)^{q-1}
 \quad(\Delta\ge0).
 \label{pr:eq:caplower}
\end{equation}
When $\Delta<0$, the second term requested in \eqref{pr:eq:renewalmain} is
zero. The two lower families belong to different powers of $W_m$ and may be
added; alternatively, the maximum of their lower bounds controls half their
sum. Together with \eqref{pr:eq:backgroundlower}, this proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

(Here $\gamma=1-(q-1)\theta_+$ as in the proof of the upper bound.)

\subsection{Why the reciprocal endpoint changes the exponent}
Away from a boundary, $q$ macroscopic parts have $q-1$ free size
coordinates. Their local weighted count is
\[
 n^{q-1}\bigl(n^{-1-\alpha}\bigr)^q=n^{-1-q\alpha}.
\]
At $n=qm$, those $q$ parts cannot vary macroscopically: they would all have
to sit at the cap. Allowing a small-part remainder of size $s$ restores only
$s^{q-1}$ choices. The unrestricted renewal coefficients obey the upper
bound $O(s^{-1-\alpha})$, so the relevant truncated moment is
$O(n^{q-1-\alpha})$. This bounds the contribution by
$O(n^{-1-(q+1)\alpha})$, an order that is actually attained by the separate
lower family with $q+1$ genuinely macroscopic parts.

The crossover occurs when
\[
 n^{-q(1+\alpha)}\Delta^{q-1}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad n^{-1-(q+1)\alpha}
\]
have the same order, equivalently
\begin{equation}
 \Delta\asymp n^{1-\alpha/(q-1)}.
 \label{pr:eq:generalwindow}
\end{equation}
This algebra is not the proof of the estimate; it is an interpretation of the
proved uniform bounds. The inequalities $q-1>\alpha$ and $\bar w<1$ are
exactly the two points that make the argument work without an extra
logarithm or a critical-renewal correction.

\section{Transferring the theorem to permutations}
\label{pr:sec:application}

\subsection{Finite shifts do not alter the order function}
The lower and upper envelopes in \eqref{pr:eq:normalizedsandwich} have
slightly different cutoffs and coefficient indices. We first remove that
bookkeeping issue.

\begin{lemma}[Bounded-shift stability]\label{pr:lem:shift}
Fix $q\ge2$ and a positive bounded interval of ratios $m/n$. For any fixed
integers $e,f$, the two expressions
\[
 \mathcal O_q(n,m),\qquad \mathcal O_q(n+e,m+f)
\]
are comparable by constants independent of $n,m$, whenever $n$ is
sufficiently large. The same is true of the renewal order expression in
\eqref{pr:eq:renewalmain}, for $0<\alpha<1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The powers of $n$ change only by bounded factors. The excess $qm-n$ changes
by the fixed amount $qf-e$. For every fixed $p\ge1$ and fixed real $c$,
\[
 (x+c)_+^p\le C_{p,c}(x_+^p+1),\qquad
 x_+^p\le C_{p,c}((x+c)_+^p+1).
\]
For $\mathcal O_q$, the extra constant is multiplied by $n^{-3(q-1)/2}$,
which is at most a constant times $n^{-q/2}$ because $q\ge2$. In the renewal
expression, it is absorbed because $q-1-\alpha>0$, as in the proof of
\cref{pr:thm:renewal}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of the uniform permutation estimate}
\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{pr:thm:cross}]
By \eqref{pr:eq:twotails}, both normalized kernels satisfy
\eqref{pr:eq:generalassump} with $\alpha=1/2$. Their masses are $3/4$ and
$3/8$, strictly below one. Apply \cref{pr:thm:renewal} to $u^-_{m-1}(n)$ and
$u^+_m(n-1)$, using slightly enlarged fixed ratio intervals if necessary to
accommodate the shifts. The resulting common order, after
\cref{pr:lem:shift}, is
\begin{equation}
 4^{-n}a_n^{(m)}\asymp
 n^{-(q+3)/2}+n^{-3q/2}\prpos{qm-n}^{q-1}.
 \label{pr:eq:countsnormalized}
\end{equation}
The lower inequality follows from the lower envelope, and the upper
inequality from the upper envelope. Finally divide by
$4^{-n}C_n\asymp n^{-3/2}$ from \eqref{pr:eq:catasymp}. This gives exactly
\eqref{pr:eq:cross}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of the staircase and its endpoints}
\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{pr:thm:staircase}]
First suppose $1/2<\theta<1$. Use \cref{pr:thm:cross} with $q=2$ and a ratio
interval containing both $1/2$ and $\theta$. For $m=\lfloor\theta n\rfloor$,
the positive excess $2m-n$ is of order $n$. The second term in
\eqref{pr:eq:cross} is therefore of order $n^{-1/2}$ and dominates $n^{-1}$.

Now suppose $0<\theta\le1/2$ and put $r=\lfloor1/\theta\rfloor\ge2$. Then
\[
 \frac1{r+1}<\theta\le\frac1r.
\]
Choose $\theta_-<\theta$ and $\theta_+>1/r$ satisfying \eqref{pr:eq:ab} with
$q=r$. If $m=\lfloor\theta n\rfloor$, then $rm-n\le0$. The second term of
\eqref{pr:eq:cross} vanishes, leaving the order $n^{-r/2}$. This also handles
$\theta=1/r$ without a limiting argument.

The count statement follows from Catalan asymptotics. Taking logarithms gives
\eqref{pr:eq:lograte}, since the logarithms of the two comparison constants
are $O(1)$. The same choices of $\theta_\pm$ work for $\theta$ in a fixed
compact subinterval of a phase, proving the asserted uniformity.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{pr:cor:scales,pr:cor:half}]
Factoring $n^{-q/2}$ from \eqref{pr:eq:cross} leaves
\[
 1+n^{-(2q-3)/2}\prpos{qm-n}^{q-1}.
\]
The exponent identity
\[
 (q-1)\left(1-\frac1{2(q-1)}\right)=\frac{2q-3}{2}
\]
gives \eqref{pr:eq:crossfactored}. All stated regimes follow by comparing the
bracketed terms. For $q=2$, the excess is $2m-n$ and the scale is $\sqrt n$,
proving \cref{pr:cor:half}. Integer rounding changes the excess by only
$O(1)$ and is covered by \cref{pr:lem:shift}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A continuum of logarithmic exponents inside a shrinking neighbourhood}
\begin{corollary}[Power-scale approach from above]\label{pr:cor:powerapproach}
Fix $q\ge2$, $c>0$, and $0<\beta<1$. Set
\[
 m_n=\left\lfloor\frac nq+c n^\beta\right\rfloor.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 p_n(m_n)=\Theta\!\left(n^{-\eta_q(\beta)}\right),\qquad
 \eta_q(\beta)=\min\left\{\frac q2,
                     (q-1)\left(\frac32-\beta\right)\right\}.
 \label{pr:eq:powerapproach}
\end{equation}
With $m_n=\lfloor n/q-c n^\beta\rfloor$, the order is always $n^{-q/2}$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The ratio $m_n/n$ tends to $1/q$, and $qm_n-n=qc n^\beta+O(1)$ in the first
case. Substitute into \eqref{pr:eq:cross}. The sum of two positive powers of
$n$ has the order of the larger term, which gives the minimum exponent. In
the second case the excess is eventually negative.
\end{proof}

For example, $m=n/2+n^{3/4}+O(1)$ gives rarity $\Theta(n^{-3/4})$, while
$m=n/3+n^{7/8}+O(1)$ gives $\Theta(n^{-5/4})$. These are genuine
intermediate logarithmic exponents, even though the fixed-fraction diagram is
a staircase.

\subsection{The exact sense of ``window''}
A window often means that a rescaled probability converges to a nonconstant
function of the scaled parameter. That convergence is not proved here. Here
the phrase \emph{order-crossover scale} has the following precise meaning:
below a positive excess of order $\mathfrak w_q(n)$ the first term controls
the order, and above it the second term eventually dominates by an unbounded
factor. The uniform comparison \eqref{pr:eq:crossfactored} proves this
statement directly. (Part~IV's question \cref{cjm:sec:mj-half} asks for a
window in the stronger sense as well; that part stays open.) [Added
1 October 2026, batch 72: it is proved at $q=2$ in Part~VII and for every
fixed $q\ge2$ in Part~VIII (\cref{hc:thm:window,gc:thm:window}).]

Likewise \eqref{pr:eq:lograte} is a pointwise logarithmic rarity law, not a
full large-deviation principle asserted without qualification. At a
reciprocal boundary the pointwise cumulative exponent jumps, and ordinary
large-deviation terminology would require care with lower semicontinuous
rate functions and the distinction between open and closed events.

\section{Interpretation, robustness, and limitations}
\label{pr:sec:interpretation}

\subsection{Why a coarse sandwich recovers an exact exponent}
The masses $3/8$ and $3/4$ and the tail constants in \eqref{pr:eq:twotails}
differ substantially. Consequently the envelopes cannot be expected to agree
to first relative order. Nevertheless, both are subcritical and both assign a
macroscopic piece of size $k$ a normalized weight of order $k^{-3/2}$. The
deficit simplex has the same dimension in both models. Those three
properties determine the powers of $n$, while the details discarded by the
upper bound and restricted by the lower construction affect constants.

This is why the method reaches the entire lower-fraction hierarchy without
resolving the complicated endpoint-state eigenvectors. It is also why the
proof stops at a two-sided comparison rather than silently promoting it to
an equivalent.

\subsection{A general transfer principle}
The same argument applies outside this permutation problem.

\begin{proposition}[Renewal-sandwich universality]\label{pr:prop:universality}
Suppose a nonnegative counting sequence $\tilde a_n^{(m)}$ is normalized by
$\lambda_\star^{-n}$, and its normalized coefficients lie between two
truncated renewal sequences satisfying \eqref{pr:eq:generalassump} with the
same $0<\alpha<1$, up to fixed shifts of $m,n$ and positive constant factors.
Suppose the unrestricted normalization satisfies
\[
 \lambda_\star^{-n}\tilde a_n\asymp n^{-1-\alpha}.
\]
Then, uniformly near each fixed reciprocal threshold $m/n=1/q$, $q\ge2$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\tilde a_n^{(m)}}{\tilde a_n}\asymp
 n^{-q\alpha}
 +n^{-(q-1)(1+\alpha)}\prpos{qm-n}^{q-1}.
 \label{pr:eq:universalprob}
\end{equation}
The associated order-crossover scale is $n^{1-\alpha/(q-1)}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{pr:thm:renewal} to both bounds, use the bounded-shift argument,
and divide by $n^{-1-\alpha}$. Equating the two terms gives the scale.
\end{proof}

The hypotheses are substantive. A generic Catalan class does not
automatically admit the required upper and lower cutoff encodings. The
proposition isolates exactly what must be proved before importing the phase
diagram into another class.

\subsection{Finite changes, subcriticality, and the tail-index restriction}
Changing finitely many kernel weights does not change the theorem's powers
as long as the total mass stays below one and the two-sided tail bounds
remain valid. More generally, the proof tolerates bounded multiplicative
changes of all sufficiently large weights. This robustness is built into
\eqref{pr:eq:generalassump}.

Subcriticality cannot simply be removed. For example,
\[
 W(z)=1-\sqrt{1-z}
\]
has nonnegative coefficients of order $k^{-3/2}$ and total mass one, but
$(1-W(z))^{-1}=(1-z)^{-1/2}$ has coefficients of order $n^{-1/2}$, not
$n^{-3/2}$. Thus \cref{pr:lem:local}, the basic input to the proof, fails at
criticality.

The condition $\alpha<1$ ensures $q-1>\alpha$ at every $q\ge2$. At
$\alpha=1$ and $q=2$ the truncated moment used in \eqref{pr:eq:criticalupper}
becomes logarithmic. The exact endpoint theorem in that regime requires a
separate statement. It is not justified by replacing $\alpha$ by one in the
displayed formulas.

\subsection{Comparison with the known upper-half profile}
When $q=2$ and $\theta>1/2$ is fixed, \cref{pr:thm:staircase} gives
$\Theta(n^{-1/2})$, consistent with but weaker than Part~IV's
\eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio}. Near $\theta=1/2$ from above, that previously
reported prefactor has the expansion
\[
 \frac{3(2\theta-1)}{\sqrt{\pi\theta(1-\theta)}}
 =\frac{12}{\sqrt\pi}\left(\theta-\frac12\right)
   +O\!\left((\theta-\tfrac12)^3\right)
\]
(this is Part~IV's \eqref{mj:eq:half-edge} with $u=1-\theta$). Substituting
$\theta-1/2=s/n$ heuristically suggests the order $s n^{-3/2}$. The present
proof establishes that order uniformly once $s\gg\sqrt n$, without assuming
that the fixed-$\theta$ equivalent can be extrapolated into a shrinking
interval. It also proves the missing $n^{-1}$ background at and below the
threshold.

\subsection{Scope of the advance}
\label{pr:sec:scope}
The results resolve the exponent part of Part~IV's proposed hierarchy
(\cref{mj:sec:q-hierarchy}) and the polynomial order and order-crossover
scale at $n/2$ (\cref{cjm:sec:mj-half,pr:sec:cjm-half}). They extend the
same analysis to every $n/q$. They do not establish the proposed functions
$\Omega_r(\theta)$, do not determine the exact constants at reciprocal
endpoints, and do not prove a limiting distribution for the macroscopic
block sizes in the actual conditioned permutation. They also leave the
fixed-$m$ component-ordering problem untouched.

The constants in the proofs depend on the fixed integer $q$. No uniformity
is claimed as $q\to\infty$, equivalently as the adjacency fraction tends to
zero. The theorem therefore does not yet connect the linear-bound regime to
fixed $m$ or to an arbitrary mesoscopic sequence $m=o(n)$.

\section{Exact checks and reproducibility}
\label{pr:sec:checks}

\subsection{What the program checks}
The delivered standard-library Python program
\path{code/07-polynomial-rarity-verify.py} performs several independent
finite checks. First, for $n\le7$, it compares a direct $132$-pattern
predicate on all permutations with the recursive Catalan generator. Second,
it compares 54 endpoint-recurrence counts with exhaustive avoider counts for
$n\le9$. Third, it generates 13,431 lower-construction objects across 60
parameter pairs, checking avoidance, the jump bound, absence of duplicate
outputs, and agreement with the lower generating function. Fourth, it checks
\[
 [x^n]\underline A_m\le a_n^{(m)}\le[x^n]\overline A_m\le C_n
\]
for 276 pairs with $2\le m\le n\le24$. Finally it reproduces the
counterexample to the invalid upper shortcut in
\cref{pr:rem:falsemajorant}.

All those assertions passed in the delivered run. The finite checks test the
implementation and the combinatorial interfaces. They do not certify
\cref{pr:thm:renewal} or any asymptotic statement; those rest on the proofs
above.

\subsection{Illustrative exact counts}
The program also computes exact integer counts for 20 fixed-fraction samples
through $n=128$ and 18 crossover samples. The CSV files retain the integers,
so every displayed probability can be reconstructed as an exact rational
number. The decimals below are rounded and are not interval certificates.

\subsection{A concrete lower-construction example}
For $m=5$, take the size-four block $(3,2,1,4)$ and the size-three block
$(2,1,3)$. Their skew sum is
\[
 (6,5,4,7,2,1,3).
\]
Its largest jump is the boundary jump $7-2=5$. Each block ends at its
maximum and starts one below it, exactly as required in
\cref{pr:lem:lower}. The skew-indecomposable cuts recover the two chosen
blocks. This tiny example exhibits the encoding used to obtain the lower
bound at every scale.

\subsection{Rebuilding and rerunning}
\label{pr:sec:reproduce}
Part~VI's files are
\begin{lstlisting}
code/07-polynomial-rarity-model.py     Exact counters, envelopes, lower-
                                       construction generator, order proxy
code/07-polynomial-rarity-verify.py    Finite tests and exact-count data
                                       (standard library)
code/07-polynomial-rarity-figures.py   The two figures of this part
                                       (NumPy, Matplotlib)
code/07-polynomial-rarity-Makefile     The manuscript's convenience targets
data/07-polynomial-rarity-*            The two exact-count CSV files and
                                       the recorded run log
figures/07-polynomial-rarity-*         The two figures (PDF and PNG)
07-polynomial-rarity-SOURCES.md        Pinned source files, literature
                                       ledger and status
\end{lstlisting}
The programs are shipped byte-identical to the delivery and still use the
delivered names: \path{verify.py} imports \texttt{model}, which in this
directory would load Part~I's different \path{code/model.py}, and writes
\path{data/phase_counts.csv}, \path{data/crossover_counts.csv} and
\path{data/verification_log.txt}; \path{figures.py} reads
\path{data/phase_counts.csv} and writes unprefixed files in
\path{figures/}; the Makefile builds an \texttt{article.tex} beside itself.
The report's README gives a recipe that restores the delivered layout in a
scratch copy; there, the manuscript's instructions apply,
\begin{verbatim}
python code/verify.py
python code/figures.py
\end{verbatim}
and rerunning overwrites only the copy's recorded outputs. The exact checks
require only Python's standard library (Python 3.10 or later); regenerating
the figures additionally requires NumPy and Matplotlib. The endpoint
recurrence used in the counting program is explained in \cref{pr:app:dp}.
The source ledger \path{07-polynomial-rarity-SOURCES.md} identifies the
exact repository files inspected; on the identifier returned by its initial
tree query it is mistaken (\cref{pr:sec:provenance}). The retained figures
make Python unnecessary for rebuilding the article.

\section{Further research questions and proposed approaches}
\label{pr:sec:research}
The order theorem separates several previously entangled questions. The
following agenda is ordered roughly from the closest analytic refinement to
more structural extensions. None of the questions in this section is
asserted to have been solved here. Several refine questions of Parts~IV
and~V; each says which.

\subsection{Exact amplitudes inside the lower phases}
\label{pr:sec:q-amplitudes}
For fixed $r\ge2$ and $1/(r+1)<\theta<1/r$, does there exist a positive
function $\Omega_r(\theta)$ such that
\begin{equation}
 p_n(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)\sim\Omega_r(\theta)n^{-r/2}?
 \label{pr:eq:amplitudequestion}
\end{equation}
\Cref{pr:thm:staircase} proves that the normalized sequence is bounded above
and away from zero, but that is not convergence. The two renewal envelopes
have different masses and different tail constants, so squeezing between
them cannot identify $\Omega_r$. A promising next step is an exact
decomposition into a finite macroscopic skeleton decorated by microscopic
avoiding blocks. One would need both unique encoding and a summable bound on
all decorations before interchanging a scaling limit with the sum over
skeletons. The known upper-half formula \eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio} is a useful
compatibility test, not a license to extrapolate. (This is the amplitude
half of Part~IV's \cref{mj:sec:q-hierarchy}, whose exponent half
\cref{pr:thm:staircase} answers.)
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 71: answered for $r=2$ in Part~VII:
$\Omega_2(\theta)=I_3(\theta)/(2\pi)$ for $1/3<\theta<1/2$, with $I_3$ the
three-piece integral \eqref{hc:eq:I3} (\cref{hc:thm:phase}). It is obtained
from an exact decomposition of the kind proposed here, the canonical
threshold parser of \cref{hc:lem:parser}. For $r\ge3$ the question stays
open (\cref{hc:sec:q-lower}).]
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 72: answered for every $r\ge2$ in Part~VIII:
$\Omega_r(\theta)=\varkappa_{r+1}I_{r+1}(\theta)$ for $1/(r+1)<\theta<1/r$,
uniformly on compact subintervals, with the capped-simplex integral
\eqref{gc:eq:integral} and $\varkappa_p=(p+1)/(2^p\pi^{(p-1)/2})$
(\cref{gc:thm:phase}). The skeleton is again the canonical parser; the new
ingredient is the endpoint-prefix count \eqref{gc:eq:accepted}.]

\subsection{Constants at the reciprocal endpoints}
\label{pr:sec:q-endpoints}
For each $q\ge2$, determine whether
\[
 \mathfrak K_q:=\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{q/2}p_n(\lfloor n/q\rfloor)
\]
exists and compute it. The theorem gives $0<\liminf\le\limsup<\infty$. It
does not exclude residue-class oscillations caused by the lattice. An exact
skeleton expansion should first establish whether changing $qm-n$ by a
bounded integer changes the normalized limit. The bounded-shift lemma
(\cref{pr:lem:shift}) proves only comparability, so an eventual proof must
genuinely sharpen that step.
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 71: answered for $q=2$ in Part~VII:
$\mathfrak K_2=6$, along all $n$, so there is no residue-class oscillation
in the limit (\cref{hc:thm:window}). For $q\ge3$ the question stays open
(\cref{hc:sec:q-lower}).]
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 72: answered for every $q\ge2$ in Part~VIII:
$\mathfrak K_q=\varkappa_{q+1}I_{q+1}(1/q)$ along all $n$, so no residue-class
oscillation survives in the limit (\cref{gc:thm:window} at $t=0$); for
example $\mathfrak K_3=5/\sqrt{2\pi}$ (\cref{gc:cor:third}).]

\subsection{Limiting crossover functions}
\label{pr:sec:q-crossover}
A stronger version of \cref{pr:thm:cross} would establish, locally uniformly
in real $t$,
\begin{equation}
 n^{q/2}p_n\!\left(\left\lfloor\frac nq+t\,\mathfrak w_q(n)\right\rfloor\right)
 \longrightarrow \mathfrak H_q(t).
 \label{pr:eq:Hquestion}
\end{equation}
The current estimates predict only $\mathfrak H_q(t)\asymp1+t_+^{q-1}$,
assuming such a limit exists. A particularly simple candidate is
\[
 \mathfrak H_q(t)=\mathfrak K_q+\mathfrak L_q t_+^{q-1},\qquad
 \mathfrak K_q,\mathfrak L_q>0.
\]
This is a proposed hypothesis, not a conclusion of the proof. It reflects
the two mechanisms in the renewal estimate: a background family and a newly
available near-cap simplex. The actual permutation decorations could require
a more complicated formula. For $q=2$, matching this candidate to
\eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio} would suggest $\mathfrak L_2=12/\sqrt\pi$, but only
after proving a suitable uniform overlap regime. Fixed-$\theta$ asymptotics
alone do not determine that slope. (For $q=2$ this is the transition
function asked for in Part~IV's \cref{cjm:sec:mj-half} and Part~V's
\cref{pr:sec:cjm-half}.)
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 71: answered for $q=2$ in Part~VII. The
candidate holds: $\mathfrak H_2(t)=6+(12/\sqrt\pi)t_+$, so
$\mathfrak K_2=6$ and $\mathfrak L_2=12/\sqrt\pi$, the suggested slope,
proved directly rather than by matching (\cref{hc:thm:window}). For $q\ge3$
the question stays open (\cref{hc:sec:q-higher}).]
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 72: answered for every $q\ge2$ in Part~VIII.
The candidate holds, $\mathfrak H_q(t)=\mathfrak K_q+\mathfrak L_qt_+^{q-1}$
with $\mathfrak L_q=(q+1)q^{5q/2-1}/(2^q\pi^{(q-1)/2}(q-1)!)$
(\cref{gc:thm:window}); at $q=3$ it is
$5/\sqrt{2\pi}+(729\sqrt3/(4\pi))t_+^2$ (\cref{gc:cor:third}).]

\subsection{The geometry of a conditioned permutation}
\label{pr:sec:q-geometry}
What does a uniform avoider conditioned on $M_n\le m$ look like when $m/n$
lies in a lower phase? The proof marks large parts in two auxiliary renewal
encodings, not in a canonical decomposition of every valid permutation. It
therefore does not prove that the conditioned permutation has a prescribed
number of macroscopic blocks. A useful objective is a canonical skeleton map
for the actual class, followed by a limit theorem for its ordered size
vector. One should distinguish the number of macroscopic blocks, their
values, their positions, and the location of the largest jump. The
reciprocal transition might then become a geometric degeneration of a
simplex of allowed block sizes. (Part~IV's \cref{mj:thm:conditional} gives
the two-block geometry for $\theta>1/2$, and its question
\cref{cjm:sec:mj-geometric} asks for the whole permutation there.)
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 71: for $1/3<\theta\le1/2$ Part~VII's canonical
parser and its three-piece endpoint table \eqref{hc:eq:chi2} identify the
leading families, but it proves no conditioned limit theorem; see
\cref{hc:sec:q-geometry}.]
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 72: partly answered in Part~VIII. In every fixed
phase the conditioned permutation has exactly $p$ macroscopic parser pieces
with probability tending to one; their ordered sizes converge to the density
$I_p(\theta)^{-1}\prod x_i^{-3/2}$, independently of $p+1$ uniform
endpoint/gap channels (\cref{gc:cor:geometry}), and in each reciprocal window
the law is a two-family mixture (\cref{gc:cor:mixture}). The positions and
values of the blocks in the permutation and the location of the largest jump
stay open (\cref{gc:sec:q-whole}).]

\subsection{Uniform relative errors and matching of scales}
\label{pr:sec:q-uniform}
Can \eqref{pr:eq:amplitudequestion} be made uniform as $\theta$ approaches a
reciprocal boundary, with an error that overlaps the scale in
\eqref{pr:eq:Hquestion}? This requires more than adding a little-$o$ term to
the present bounds. The small-part suppression lemma deliberately chooses a
very small fixed cutoff and does not optimize the resulting constants. A
sharper method would track the cutoff through a dominated-convergence
argument and isolate the contribution of intermediate-sized parts. A
successful theorem should also specify the effect of rounding $m$ and give a
genuinely relative, rather than merely additive, error.
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 71: at $q=2$ Part~VII's limits come without an
error rate; see \cref{hc:sec:q-errors}.]
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 72: so do Part~VIII's for every $q$; see
\cref{gc:sec:q-rate}.]

\subsection{Growing reciprocal index and the spectral regime}
\label{pr:sec:q-growing}
The theorem treats fixed $q$, so it does not apply uniformly when
$m/n\to0$. A next scale is $q=q(n)\to\infty$ slowly, or equivalently an
adjacency bound tending to infinity but sublinear in $n$. Which growth rates
of $q$ preserve a few-macroscopic-pieces description? When does a saddle
point or the fixed-$m$ spectral radius become the correct language? The
constants hidden in the proof depend strongly on $q$, on the available ratio
interval, and on the chosen small-part cutoff. Keeping those dependencies
explicit is a prerequisite for connecting the present theorem with the
report's fixed-bound growth-constant theory (Parts~I and~II). (This is
Part~IV's question \cref{pr:sec:mj-bridge}, and Part~III's
\cref{lj:sec:q-reconnect} from the other end.)

\subsection{Effective bounds and improved lower subclasses}
\label{pr:sec:q-effective}
The existence proof can, in principle, be made effective after supplying
explicit Catalan bounds and explicit choices of $A,L,\varepsilon,\delta$.
Such constants would probably be far from sharp. It would be useful to
produce certified, practical inequalities for $p_n(m)$ over finite ranges.
The lower construction used here fixes the first deficiency at at most one.
Allowing a bounded but larger deficiency enlarges the subclass, while still
admitting exact block counts (Part~I's $K_{m,d}$, \eqref{eq:K}). How much can
the constants improve before the cutoff loss starts to compete with the
crossover scale? This is an optimization problem about rigorous
inequalities, distinct from proving an exact asymptotic amplitude.

\subsection{Other pattern classes and cutoff models}
\label{pr:sec:q-classes}
\Cref{pr:prop:universality} supplies a reusable criterion, but not an
automatic result for every Catalan class. For another permutation class,
tree family, or weighted composition model, identify a genuine
cutoff-respecting injection and a coefficientwise upper encoding with the
same tail index. Merely having unrestricted growth $\lambda_\star^n n^{-3/2}$
is not sufficient. The central question is whether the local statistic being
bounded can be translated into a cap on every part with only bounded shifts.
Establishing that translation would turn the present analytic theorem into a
phase-diagram theorem for another concrete model. (Compare Part~V's
\cref{pr:sec:cjm-other}.)

\subsection{Other tail indices and critical renewal mass}
\label{pr:sec:q-tails}
Two adjacent analytic problems are especially natural. At $\alpha=1$, the
moment at the first reciprocal threshold is borderline and may introduce
logarithmic factors. At $\bar w=1$, the renewal sequence is critical and the
local tail lemma used here fails. Determine the correct two-term estimates in
those regimes, including their endpoint orders and crossover scales. The two
changes should initially be studied separately: altering the tail index
affects moment integrability, whereas altering the total mass changes the
number of microscopic parts. There is no reason for a single formal
substitution to handle both.

\subsection{A staged formalization plan}
\label{pr:sec:q-formal}
A feasible formalization should separate three interfaces. The finite
combinatorial layer consists of maximum decomposition, skew sums, unique
skew-indecomposable factorization, and the injection behind
$\underline A_m$. The formal-series layer consists of nonnegative
coefficientwise inequalities and the identity \eqref{pr:eq:markedexpansion}.
The analytic layer consists of the geometric-polynomial summation in
\cref{pr:lem:local}, the exponential tilt in \cref{pr:lem:small}, and the
explicit composition bounds in \cref{pr:thm:renewal}. Only after those layers
are connected should an audit assert the main asymptotic theorem. The
endpoint dynamic program and its test cases would be useful executable
specifications, but they are not substitutes for any of these proof
obligations. (Parts~IV and~V propose formalization targets in
\cref{cjm:sec:mj-formal,pr:sec:cjm-formal}; no Lean or Rocq declaration for
any part of this report exists.)

\section{Conclusion}
\label{pr:sec:conclusion}
The lower-fraction regime has a simple logarithmic structure: each time the
adjacency fraction crosses a reciprocal threshold from above, the rarity
gains another factor $n^{-1/2}$. At the threshold itself the rarer exponent
already applies. The transition from the threshold background to the
neighbouring larger probability occurs at a fractional-power scale that
depends on the reciprocal index.

The proof is economical in its dependence on previous work. It does not
require the full endpoint-state spectrum, a conjectured exact asymptotic
profile, or any unverified assertion elsewhere in the report. Two elementary
encodings place the counting sequence between subcritical renewal
coefficients with the same tail exponent. A local composition argument then
transfers a robust phase diagram through that sandwich. What remains
difficult is no longer identifying the powers: it is recovering the exact
amplitudes and the structure of the conditioned objects.

\section{The exact endpoint recurrence used in the checks}
\label{pr:app:dp}
This section was the manuscript's Appendix~A. It explains the implemented
dynamic program. The recurrence is due to Mayama--Akita~\cite{MayamaAkita}
and is Part~I's \cref{prop:endpoint}, in the coefficient form Part~IV prints
as \eqref{mj:eq:DP}, with Part~IV's shortcut \eqref{mj:eq:unrestrictedDP};
Part~V derives it again in \cref{cjm:app:recurrence}. The delivered
implementation is a separately packaged adaptation of Part~IV's counter
\path{code/05-macroscopic-deficits-model.py}. The manuscript's derivation is
kept here, in Parts~IV and~V's notation (\cref{pr:tab:notation}), because its
checks were built on it; its first-entry argument differs from those of
Parts~I, III and~V. No correctness claim for the main asymptotic theorem is
delegated to this program.

\subsection{First-entry counts}
For a permutation of size $n$, call $n-\pi_1$ its first deficiency. For
$0\le d<n$, the number of unrestricted avoiders with first deficiency exactly
$d$ is
\begin{equation}
 N_{n,n-d}=\frac{d+1}{n}\binom{2n-d-2}{n-1},
 \label{pr:eq:firstentry}
\end{equation}
Part~III's \eqref{lj:eq:first-count} with first entry $f=n-d$. Here is a
direct derivation. Fix the first entry $f=n-d$. The $d$ entries larger than
$f$ must occur in increasing order: a decreasing pair of them together with
the first entry would form $132$. These $d$ entries divide the remaining
$f-1$ smaller entries into $d+1$ gaps. All entries in an earlier gap must
exceed those in a later gap, since otherwise the separating larger entry
completes a $132$ pattern. Conversely, these conditions, with an avoiding
permutation inside each gap, are sufficient. Thus
\[
 N_{n,n-d}=[x^{n-d-1}]\Cat(x)^{d+1}.
\]
The generalized Catalan coefficient identity
\[
 [x^r]\Cat(x)^k=\frac{k}{2r+k}\binom{2r+k}{r}
 \qquad(k\ge1,\ r\ge0)
\]
follows from $Y=x(1+Y)^2$, $Y=\Cat-1$, by formal coefficient inversion (or by
the standard ballot decomposition); here $r$ and $k$ are local indices.
Substitution of $r=n-d-1$, $k=d+1$ and cancellation of factorials gives
\eqref{pr:eq:firstentry}. This also proves its integrality.

Write
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal N_n(u)=\sum_{d=0}^{\min(u,n-1)} N_{n,n-d}\qquad(n\ge1,\ u\ge0),
 \label{pr:eq:hdef}
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal N_n(u)=0$ for $u<0$, and $\mathcal N_0(u)=1$ for $u\ge0$ (Part~V's
\eqref{cjm:eq:h-count}). The implementation precomputes these cumulative
counts using integers and checks $\mathcal N_n(n-1)=C_n$ for every row it
constructs.

\subsection{Unrestricted two-endpoint counts}
Let $T^{\mathrm{free}}_{u,v}(n)$ count unrestricted avoiders with first
deficiency at most $u$ and last deficiency at most $v$, where $u,v\ge0$. For
$n\ge2$,
\begin{equation}
 T^{\mathrm{free}}_{u,v}(n)=\mathcal N_{n-1}(u-1)
 +\sum_{j=1}^{\min(v,n-1)}C_{n-j-1}\mathcal N_j(u).
 \label{pr:eq:freeendpoints}
\end{equation}
(This is Part~IV's shortcut \eqref{mj:eq:unrestrictedDP}, which holds for
$T^{(m)}_{u,v}(n)$ when $n\le m+1$.) To prove this, first consider a final
maximum. Removing it leaves an arbitrary avoider of size $n-1$, whose first
deficiency is one less than the original one. This gives the first term.

Otherwise let the final value be $n-j$, with $j\ge1$. Every larger value must
occur before every smaller value: a smaller value followed by a larger one
and then by $n-j$ would be a $132$ pattern. Hence the permutation consists of
an avoiding block of the $j$ larger values, an avoiding block of the $n-j-1$
smaller values, and the last entry. The first deficiency is the first
deficiency of the larger block; the last deficiency is $j$. The factors in
the sum follow. When $n=1$, the unrestricted count is one for all
nonnegative thresholds.

\subsection{The adjacency-restricted recurrence}
Fix $m$. Let $T^{(m)}_{u,v}(n)$ count the size-$n$ avoiders with jump bound
$m$ and first and last deficiencies at most $u,v$. Negative thresholds give
zero; $T^{(m)}_{u,v}(1)=1$ for nonnegative thresholds. Thresholds larger
than $n-1$ can be clipped to $n-1$.

For $n\ge2$ the exact recurrence is
\begin{equation}
 T^{(m)}_{u,v}(n)=T^{(m)}_{u-1,m-1}(n-1)
 +\sum_{k=1}^{\min(m,n-1)}c_{k,u}\,T^{(m)}_{m-k,v-k}(n-k),
 \label{pr:eq:exactdp}
\end{equation}
for $u,v\ge0$, where
\begin{equation}
 c_{1,u}=1,\qquad c_{k,u}=\mathcal N_{k-1}(u-1)\quad(k\ge2);
 \label{pr:eq:coeffdp}
\end{equation}
this is \eqref{mj:eq:DP}. The first term places the maximum at the end. The
last jump is one plus the last deficiency in the shorter permutation, so
that deficiency must be at most $m-1$. The first deficiency drops by one when
the maximum is removed.

For a maximum at position $k<n$, its left block has size $k-1$, and
$k\le m$. All internal jumps and the jump from the left block to the maximum
are automatically at most $m$. The first-deficiency restriction on that block
is $u-1$, giving $c_{k,u}$. The first deficiency of the right block must be at
most $m-k$, because the jump after the maximum is $k$ plus that deficiency.
The original last deficiency is $k$ plus the right block's last deficiency.
These are exactly the two thresholds in the summand. The case $k=1$ has an
empty left block and original first deficiency zero.

If $n\le m+1$, the adjacency restriction is vacuous, and
\eqref{pr:eq:freeendpoints} provides a useful shortcut. The desired count is
\[
 a_n^{(m)}=T^{(m)}_{n-1,n-1}(n).
\]
The recursion always reduces $n$, so memoization terminates. All operations
are sums, products, and exact integer divisions in \eqref{pr:eq:firstentry};
no floating-point number affects a count.

\section{Proof audit and reproducibility boundary}
\label{pr:app:audit}
This section was the manuscript's Appendix~B.

\subsection{Dependency chain}
The logical chain of the main result is
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{maximum decomposition and explicit skew-block injection}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{coefficientwise upper and lower renewal envelopes}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{local subcritical tail bound and small-part suppression}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{uniform local truncated-renewal theorem}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{bounded-shift transfer and Catalan normalization}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{staircase exponents and order-crossover scales}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
The endpoint dynamic program is not on this chain. It independently tests
the finite combinatorial interfaces and generates the illustrations.

\subsection{Quantifiers that must not be weakened}
The constants in \cref{pr:thm:renewal} are uniform over all integer $m$ in
one fixed ratio interval. That uniformity, not a collection of fixed-$\theta$
estimates, is what permits the crossover conclusions. The cutoff
$\varepsilon$ is chosen after the desired suppression exponent and then held
fixed as $n$ grows. Consequently the number of macroscopic-part cases is
finite and does not introduce a hidden power of $\log n$.

The lower power bound on $w_k$ is needed for every sufficiently large integer
$k$, not just along a subsequence. This ensures that every composition in
the lower families has positive weight and prevents an unexamined
lattice-periodicity obstruction. The shifts of the cutoff and coefficient
index in \eqref{pr:eq:normalizedsandwich} are checked explicitly. Finally,
$q$ is fixed throughout the theorem; letting it grow would require new
estimates.

\subsection{What the delivered tests establish}
The checks establish exact equalities or inequalities only for the listed
finite ranges. They provide evidence that the endpoint recurrence, block
generator, and coefficient recursions have been implemented consistently.
They do not establish eventual bounds, uniform asymptotics, the existence of
limits, independent peer review, or a machine-checked proof. The manuscript
was prepared with AI assistance and should be subjected to independent
mathematical review before any stronger status is assigned.

The delivery contained the \LaTeX{} source, compiled PDF, vector figures,
plotting source, exact-count CSV files, verification program and its run log,
source ledger, and a SHA-256 manifest; the source, the PDF and the manifest
are not shipped (\cref{pr:sec:source}). A rebuild does not require network
access once the usual \LaTeX{} packages are installed. Regenerating the
figures additionally requires NumPy and Matplotlib; rerunning the exact
checks does not.

 and the endpoints. The endpoint recurrence of \cref{pr:app:dp} is Part~I's,
 as printed by Parts~IV and~V; its derivation is kept, marked. The masses

 Part~I's \eqref{eq:Bd} and \eqref{eq:cd-tail} at $d=1$; this identification
 is the merge's.
\item \emph{What Part~VI sharpens.} \Cref{pr:thm:staircase} supplies the
 upper bounds missing beside \cref{mj:thm:lower}, and for $0<\theta\le1/2$
 improves \cref{mj:thm:profile}'s $o(n^{-1/2})$ to an exact order. It is
 weaker than \cref{mj:thm:profile} for $\theta>1/2$, which it does not use.
 Nothing in Parts~I--V is refuted.
