\section{Statements}
\label{gc:sec:results}
Let $C_n=(n+1)^{-1}\binom{2n}{n}$ and let $\pi$ be uniform in $\Av_n(132)$. Set
\[
 M_n=\max_{1\le i<n}|\pi_{i+1}-\pi_i|,
 \qquad p_n(m)=\ljP(M_n\le m),
\]
with an empty maximum equal to zero (Part~VI's \eqref{pr:eq:objects} and
\eqref{pr:eq:prob}). Part~VI proves the polynomial rarity orders and the
reciprocal window scales (\cref{pr:thm:staircase,pr:thm:cross,pr:cor:scales}),
and Part~VII the exact half-size crossover and the first sub-half-size
amplitude (\cref{hc:thm:window,hc:thm:phase}). Part~VII's
\cref{hc:sec:q-lower,hc:sec:q-higher,hc:sec:q-geometry} leave the higher
amplitudes, crossover functions and conditioned piece laws open; Part~VIII
addresses those questions.

For $p\ge2$ define
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:integral}
 I_p(\theta)=
 \int_{\substack{x_1+\cdots+x_p=1\\0<x_i\le\theta}}
       \prod_{i=1}^p x_i^{-3/2}\,\dd x_1\cdots\,\dd x_{p-1},
 \qquad \varkappa_p=\frac{p+1}{2^p\pi^{(p-1)/2}}.
\end{equation}
Write $\mathcal D_p(\theta)$ for the integration domain. The measure is
coordinate measure, not Euclidean surface area. ($I_3$ is Part~VII's
\eqref{hc:eq:I3}.)

\begin{theorem}[Every fixed phase amplitude]\label{gc:thm:phase}
For fixed $p\ge2$ and $1/p<\theta<1/(p-1)$,
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:phase}
 p_n(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)
 \sim\varkappa_pI_p(\theta)\,n^{-(p-1)/2}.
\end{equation}
The convergence is uniform on compact subintervals of that open phase. For
$p\ge3$, the same equivalent holds at $\theta=1/(p-1)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Every fixed reciprocal crossover]\label{gc:thm:window}
Fix an integer $r\ge2$, and put
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak K_r&=\varkappa_{r+1}I_{r+1}(1/r),\label{gc:eq:B}\\
 \mathfrak L_r&=\frac{(r+1)r^{5r/2-1}}
             {2^r\pi^{(r-1)/2}(r-1)!}.\label{gc:eq:A}
\end{align}
Then, locally uniformly for $t\in\R$,
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:window}
 n^{r/2}p_n\!\left(
   \left\lfloor\frac nr+t\,n^{\,1-1/[2(r-1)]}\right\rfloor\right)
 \longrightarrow\mathfrak K_r+\mathfrak L_r\prpos t^{\,r-1}.
\end{equation}
The integral defining $\mathfrak K_r$ is finite and positive.
\end{theorem}

The scale $n^{1-1/[2(r-1)]}$ is Part~VI's $\mathfrak w_r(n)$ of
\eqref{pr:eq:window}, so \eqref{gc:eq:window} is \eqref{pr:eq:Hquestion} with
$\mathfrak H_r(t)=\mathfrak K_r+\mathfrak L_rt_+^{r-1}$.

The $p=2$ phase recovers Part~IV, since
\[
 I_2(\theta)=\frac{4(2\theta-1)}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)}},
 \qquad \varkappa_2I_2(\theta)
       =\frac{3(2\theta-1)}{\sqrt{\pi\theta(1-\theta)}}.
\]
The $p=3$ phase and $r=2$ window are Part~VII's results. The new amplitude
cases begin at $p=4$ and the new windows at $r=3$; the common formula includes
the known cases consistently. All constants and estimates may depend on the
fixed index. Growing-index and quantitative convergence questions remain
outside the theorem.

\begin{remark}[Merge note: the first cases are Parts~IV and~VII]
\label{gc:rem:cases}
At $p=2$, \eqref{gc:eq:phase} is Part~IV's \eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio}
(\cref{mj:thm:profile}), whose split integral is $I(u)=I_2(1-u)$ by
\eqref{mj:eq:Iu}. At $p=3$, $\varkappa_3=4/(8\pi)=1/(2\pi)$, so
\eqref{gc:eq:phase} is Part~VII's \cref{hc:thm:phase}, including its endpoint
$\theta=1/2$. At $r=2$, $\mathfrak K_2=\varkappa_3I_3(1/2)=12\pi/(2\pi)=6$ and
$\mathfrak L_2=3\cdot2^4/(4\sqrt\pi)=12/\sqrt\pi$, so \eqref{gc:eq:window} is
Part~VII's \cref{hc:thm:window}. These three theorems are printed once, in
their own Parts, and are cited here as the first cases; their proofs are the
explicit first-case routes of the general proof below. The manuscript proves
the general theorems without importing them. The merge checked the three
reductions symbolically and, at $\theta=0.6$, $0.75$, $0.8$ and $0.9$,
numerically.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Merge note: the slope is the edge of the next phase]
\label{gc:rem:matching}
The slope $\mathfrak L_r$ is what the $r$-piece amplitude predicts at its lower
edge. Just above $1/r$ the domain $\mathcal D_r(\theta)$ is a simplex of
deficits $\theta-x_i\ge0$ with sum $r\theta-1$, on which the integrand is
$r^{3r/2}+o(1)$, so
\[
 I_r(1/r+\delta)\sim\frac{r^{3r/2}(r\delta)^{r-1}}{(r-1)!}\qquad(\delta\downarrow0).
\]
Put $\delta=t\,n^{-1/[2(r-1)]}$ in \eqref{gc:eq:phase} at $p=r$ and
multiply by $n^{r/2}$: the result is
$\varkappa_rr^{3r/2}r^{r-1}t^{r-1}/(r-1)!$, which is $\mathfrak L_rt^{r-1}$; this is the manuscript's calculation in
\cref{gc:sec:shrinking}. No theorem licenses the substitution, so this is a
consistency check, not a proof, exactly as for Part~VII's slope at $q=2$
(\cref{hc:sec:answer}). A ProveIt check made for this write-up confirmed the
displayed asymptotic of $I_r$ numerically for $r=2,3,4$ (ratios
$1+O(\delta)$ at $\delta=10^{-3},10^{-4},10^{-5}$).
\end{remark}

\section{Catalan interfaces and the exact parser}
\label{gc:sec:interfaces}
The manuscript recalls the interfaces of Part~VII ``to specify the counting
and normalization completely'', and says itself that ``these parser and
endpoint interfaces are already in the source''. They are printed once, in
Part~VII, and used here by label:
\begin{itemize}
\item the Catalan bounds \eqref{hc:eq:cat} for $c_j=4^{-j}C_j$ (Part~IV's
 \eqref{mj:eq:catalan-bounds});
\item the unique maximum decomposition $\pi=(\sigma+b)N\tau$, $a=|\sigma|$,
 $b=|\tau|$, $a+b+1=N$, and its consequence $a+1\le m$ when $b>0$ and all jumps
 are at most $m$ (\cref{hc:sec:interfaces});
\item the endpoint facts for an unrestricted avoiding block of size $N$ with
 first and last entries $F$, $L$: $\ljP(N-F\ge u)\le(u+2)2^{-u}$, the last-entry
 count $C_{N-\ell}C_{\ell-1}$ and the equal limiting atoms of $L/N$ at $0$
 and $1$, that is \eqref{hc:eq:J}, \eqref{hc:eq:L} and \eqref{hc:eq:halfmass},
 uniform in their displayed ranges (\cref{hc:lem:endpoints});
\item the operations $R_\beta$ (cost $k=|\beta|+1$; it adds $k$ to the last
 deficiency) and $A$ (cost one; allowed exactly when the previous last
 deficiency plus one is at most $m$, after which it resets it to zero), and
 the canonical threshold parser \cref{hc:lem:parser}: an $m$-bounded avoider
 of size $n>m$ is uniquely an outer decorated word in $R,A$ applied to a
 terminal core with children of sizes
 \begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:terminal}
  a,b\le m,\qquad a+b+1>m,
 \end{equation}
 every outer $R$ has cost at most $m$, and accepted reconstruction is
 injective;
\item the normalized word masses $\mathcal W(1/4)=4$,
 $\mathcal W_R(1/4)=\mathcal W_A(1/4)=2$ of \eqref{hc:eq:masses}, and the full
 word series $\Cat(z)^2$, whose cost-$s$ coefficient is $C_{s+1}$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{remark}[Merge note: what the manuscript recalls]
The manuscript's equations for these interfaces (its
\texttt{eq:catalan}, \texttt{eq:first}, \texttt{eq:last},
\texttt{eq:lastlimit}, \texttt{lem:parser} and \texttt{eq:wordmass}) are
condensed restatements, partly near-verbatim, of
\eqref{hc:eq:cat}, \eqref{hc:eq:J}, \eqref{hc:eq:L}, \eqref{hc:eq:halfmass},
\cref{hc:lem:parser} and \eqref{hc:eq:masses}, with the same proofs (the
Catalan-triangle first-entry count $\frac{j+1}{N}\binom{2N-j-2}{N-1}$ and its
bound $(j+1)2^{-j-1}$, the $132$-argument for the last entry, and the
parser's rule of following a child larger than $m$); it writes $v$ for
Part~VII's $r$ in \eqref{hc:eq:halfmass} and $W$, $W_R$, $W_A$ for the masses.
Only the terminal condition \eqref{gc:eq:terminal} is displayed again, because
the later sections use it.
\end{remark}

\section{Small-word estimates and counting bounds}
\label{gc:sec:smallwords}
Fix a small cutoff $\varepsilon>0$. An outer $R$ is called macroscopic when its
cost exceeds $\varepsilon n$. The other letters form small-word gaps with
normalized generating function
\[
 S_n(z)=\frac z4+\sum_{1\le k\le\lfloor\varepsilon n\rfloor}
                     C_{k-1}4^{-k}z^k,\qquad T_n(z)=(1-S_n(z))^{-1}.
\]
There are at most $1/\varepsilon$ macroscopic outer letters. For each fixed
gap count $g$, write $T^{(g)}_s=[z^s]T_n(z)^g$.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform word bounds]\label{gc:lem:words}
For fixed $g$ and integers $\ell\ge1$,
\begin{align}
 T_n(1)^g&\le4^g,&T^{(g)}_s&\le K_g(s+1)^{-3/2},\label{gc:eq:local}\\
 \sum_{s>H}T^{(g)}_s&=O_g((H+1)^{-1/2}),&
 \sum_{s\le x}s^\ell T^{(g)}_s&=O_{g,\ell}((x+1)^{\ell-1/2}).\label{gc:eq:moments}
\end{align}
For prescribed $c,A_0>0$, choosing $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small gives
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:tilt}
 T^{(g)}_s=O_g(n^{-A_0})\qquad(s\ge cn).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The letter mass is at most $3/4$. In a word of length $l$ and cost $s$, a
largest letter has size at least $s/l$ and weight at most $Kl^{3/2}s^{-3/2}$.
Marking it gives at most $l$ choices, while the other letters have total mass
at most $(3/4)^{l-1}$. Summing with the polynomial multiplicity
$\binom{l+g-1}{g-1}$ proves \eqref{gc:eq:local}; summation gives
\eqref{gc:eq:moments}.

For \eqref{gc:eq:tilt}, choose $L>A_0/c$ and $L\varepsilon<1/2$. At
$\lambda=L\log n/n$,
\[
 S_n(e^\lambda)-S_n(1)
 \le\lambda e^{\lambda\varepsilon n}S_n'(1)
 =O((\log n)n^{L\varepsilon-1/2})=o(1).
\]
Thus $T_n(e^\lambda)^g$ stays bounded, and coefficient positivity yields the
claim. The choice of $\varepsilon$ works for every fixed finite set of gap
counts.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Merge note: the word bounds of Parts~VI and~VII]
The first three bounds and \eqref{gc:eq:tilt} are Part~VII's
\eqref{hc:eq:massbound}, \eqref{hc:eq:local} and \eqref{hc:eq:suppress} with
$h=g$ (Part~VI's \cref{pr:lem:local,pr:lem:small} for the upper kernel, tail
index $1/2$), with the same proofs, here condensed; the tail sum is a summation
of \eqref{hc:eq:local}. New is the moment bound in \eqref{gc:eq:moments} for
every order $\ell\ge1$, the analogue for $T_n^g$ of Part~VI's partial-sum
moment bound \eqref{pr:eq:momentbound}; at $\ell=1$ and $x=\varepsilon n$ it
is a truncated form of Part~VII's \eqref{hc:eq:moment}. The higher orders are
what the shrinking simplex at $1/r$ needs (\cref{gc:sec:shrinking}).
\end{remark}

When applying \eqref{gc:eq:tilt}, sum all remaining normalized decoration and
terminal weights without the total-size constraint. With $j$ macroscopic outer
letters that mass is at most a fixed constant times $(1/2)^j$. The word cost is
then determined by the remaining sizes. Division by $c_n$ loses only
$O(n^{3/2})$. A fixed exponent $A_0$ larger than the desired rare-event scaling
plus $3/2$ therefore suffices; it need not grow with $j$. Constants may depend
on the fixed cutoff, and $n$ tends to infinity before that cutoff is removed.

[Merge note. The bound ``a fixed constant times $(1/2)^j$'' is asserted
without proof, as in Part~VII; Part~VII's editorial \cref{hc:rem:jbound}
supplies it: each macroscopic $R$ letter carries total normalized mass at most
$\tfrac14\Cat(1/4)=\tfrac12$ and the terminal core at most
$\tfrac14\Cat(1/4)^2=1$. The manuscript's wording, ``decoration and terminal
weights'', is the correct reading that the remark reconstructs, and because the
word cost is then determined by the remaining sizes, it needs no summation over
the gap cost; that is why the exponent condition here, $A_0$ larger than the
rare-event scaling plus $3/2$, is sharper than the $n^{1-A_0}$ of
\cref{hc:rem:jbound}.]

Two elementary family bounds will be used repeatedly. If there are $\ell$
genuine macroscopic pieces and both terminal children are large, their Catalan
factors and the $\ell-1$ free sizes give
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:many}
 O_\varepsilon(n^{-3\ell/2}n^{\ell-1}n^{3/2})
 =O_\varepsilon(n^{-(\ell-1)/2})
\end{equation}
after summing the bounded gap mass. If a terminal child has size
$a\le\varepsilon n$, its partner satisfies Part~VII's \eqref{hc:eq:capinterval},
$m-a\le b\le m$, so it has at most $a+1$ choices. With $\ell$ genuine large
pieces, the remaining count is $O(n^{\ell-2}(a+1))$. Since
$\sum_{a\le\varepsilon n}(a+1)c_a=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon n})$,
the corresponding probability is
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:manysmall}
 O_\varepsilon(n^{-\ell/2}).
\end{equation}
This count has $\ell+1$ nominal pieces including the small terminal child; it
must not be confused with \eqref{gc:eq:many}. For sufficiently small
$\varepsilon$, both terminal children cannot be small by
\eqref{gc:eq:terminal}.

[Merge note. With $\ell=j+2$ pieces ($j$ large outer letters and two large
terminal children), \eqref{gc:eq:many} is Part~VII's \eqref{hc:eq:many}, and
\eqref{gc:eq:manysmall} its small-child variant; the manuscript's
\texttt{eq:partner} is \eqref{hc:eq:capinterval} and is not displayed
again.]

\section{The endpoint-prefix count and phase amplitudes}
\label{gc:sec:prefix}
Suppose $p$ macroscopic pieces carry the total size. There are $p-2$ outer $R$
decorations, two terminal children, and $p-1$ intervening small-word gaps,
ordered from the core outward. In the minimal $p$-piece phase,
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:pairs}
 x_i+x_j\ge1-(p-2)\theta>\theta.
\end{equation}
In particular the terminal pair exceeds the cap.

For fixed gap words and pieces in the domain interior, all first-deficiency
constraints are negligible by \eqref{hc:eq:J}; cap-boundary strips with growing
slack remove the remaining joins. Only the terminal-right last-endpoint mode
matters, classified as low or high according as $L(\tau)\le b/2$ or
$L(\tau)>b/2$. Conditional on these modes, respectively $L(\tau)$ or
$b-L(\tau)$ is tight by \eqref{hc:eq:halfmass}. The following acceptance rules
therefore hold with probability $1-o(1)$, uniformly on the retained interior.
In the low mode the initial last deficiency exceeds the cap, and no append is
possible in any gap. In the high mode the initial deficiency is the left
terminal size up to a tight error. An append in the first gap resets it. Each
next macroscopic $R$ adds its own cost, which is below the cap. If an append
gap is skipped, any later first append would encounter two macroscopic sizes,
forbidden by \eqref{gc:eq:pairs}. Once the first append within a fixed gap is
accepted, its remaining bounded costs introduce no new macroscopic constraint.
Therefore append-containing gaps form an initial contiguous prefix of length
$0,\ldots,p-1$.

There is one low-endpoint channel and $p$ high-endpoint channels. Each endpoint
mode has limiting mass $1/2$, and each gap type has mass two by
\eqref{hc:eq:masses}. The exact limiting accepted normalized gap mass is
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:accepted}
 \frac12(1+p)2^{p-1}=2^{p-2}(p+1).
\end{equation}
No permutation factor is present: the parser orders the outer decorations and
distinguishes the two terminal children.

For fixed words of total cost $s$, the Catalan product divided by $C_n$ is
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:macro}
 4^{-s-(p-1)}\pi^{-(p-1)/2}n^{-3(p-1)/2}
       \prod_{i=1}^p x_i^{-3/2}(1+o(1)).
\end{equation}
There are $n^{p-1}$ cells. Multiplying the Riemann integral by
\eqref{gc:eq:accepted} yields $\varkappa_pI_p(\theta)$.

Here are the exclusions making this a full proof in the phase interior. Fewer
than $p$ genuine large pieces leave positive linear word cost, because
$\theta<1/(p-1)$; choose the cutoff below that phase margin and use
\eqref{gc:eq:tilt}. Exactly $p$ pieces with both terminal children large give
the preceding calculation. They are bounded below by a fixed positive fraction
of $n$ when the word cost is sufficiently small, since $1-(p-1)\theta>0$. A
fixed word-cost cutoff can be removed by \eqref{gc:eq:moments}. Families with
more pieces are smaller by at least $n^{-1/2}$ using \eqref{gc:eq:many}; those
with a microscopic terminal child are handled by \eqref{gc:eq:manysmall}, or
require linear word cost if fewer than $p$ genuine pieces remain. These cases
exhaust the parser. The same margins hold uniformly on compact phase
subintervals, proving \eqref{gc:eq:phase} there.

\begin{remark}[Merge note: the masses $3$ and $8$]
\label{gc:rem:masses}
At $p=2$ the accepted mass \eqref{gc:eq:accepted} is $3$, Part~IV's word mass
\eqref{mj:eq:wordmass} and the sum $2+\tfrac12\cdot2$ of Part~VII's
\eqref{hc:eq:ramp}; the three channels (low; high with an empty or a one-gap
prefix) are Part~IV's three equally weighted endpoint channels
(\cref{mj:cor:conditional}). At $p=3$ it is $8$, Part~VII's \eqref{hc:eq:8}:
with $w_2$ the gap next to the core, the low channel and the high channel with
the empty prefix both require that neither gap contain an $A$ (weight
$\tfrac12+\tfrac12=1$), and the prefixes of lengths one and two together
require an $A$ in $w_2$ with $w_1$ arbitrary (weight $\tfrac12$), which is
Part~VII's table \eqref{hc:eq:chi2}. Equation~\eqref{gc:eq:macro} at $p=3$ is
Part~VII's \eqref{hc:eq:tripleweight}. The prefix rule, and hence the mass
$2^{p-2}(p+1)$, is new for $p\ge4$.
\end{remark}

\section{The shrinking contribution at a reciprocal threshold}
\label{gc:sec:shrinking}
Fix $r\ge2$, $|t|\le B$, and
\[
 m=\left\lfloor n/r+t\,n^{1-1/[2(r-1)]}\right\rfloor,\qquad
 \Delta=rm-n=rt\,n^{1-1/[2(r-1)]}+O(1).
\]
For $r$ genuine pieces and total small-word cost $s$, cap deficits have sum
$\Delta+s+1$. Their number is bounded by $K_r(\Delta_++s+1)^{r-1}$. For
$s\le cn$ with $c$ sufficiently small, every piece is bounded below by a fixed
positive multiple of $n$. Thus, after multiplication by $n^{r/2}$, the
contribution has prefactor $O(n^{-r+3/2})$.

Choose a small word-cost fraction $\varepsilon'>0$, then choose the
macroscopic-letter cutoff $\varepsilon$ sufficiently smaller so that
\eqref{gc:eq:tilt} applies for costs at least $\varepsilon'n$. For a fixed
cutoff $H$, the intermediate word-cost tail is at most
\begin{align}
 &K n^{-r+3/2}\sum_{H<s\le\varepsilon'n}
       T^{(g)}_s(\Delta_++s+1)^{r-1}\notag\\
 &\hspace{15mm}\le K_B(H+1)^{-1/2}
                  +K_r(\varepsilon')^{r-3/2}+o(1).\label{gc:eq:shrinkingtail}
\end{align}
This uses $(u+v)^{r-1}\le K_r(u^{r-1}+v^{r-1})$,
$\Delta_+^{r-1}=O_B(n^{r-3/2})$, and \eqref{gc:eq:moments}. Larger costs are
negligible by \eqref{gc:eq:tilt}, with $A_0>r/2+3/2$. There is no circular
dependence on the number of macroscopic letters.

For fixed words and $t>0$, the deficit simplex has
$\Delta^{r-1}/(r-1)!\,(1+o(1))$ lattice points. Delete strips of width $h_n$ at
each cap face, where $h_n\to\infty$ and $h_n=o(\Delta)$. Their relative count
tends to zero. The endpoint-prefix argument applies on the remainder, and all
scaled pieces tend to $1/r$. Hence the limit of this family's contribution is
\[
 \varkappa_rr^{3r/2}\frac{(rt)^{r-1}}{(r-1)!}
 =\mathfrak L_rt^{r-1}.
\]
For $t<0$ no fixed-word configuration remains; at $t=0$ at most a bounded
number of compositions remain for each fixed word. Both contributions vanish
after scaling. Bound \eqref{gc:eq:shrinkingtail} removes nonfixed words and
proves the contribution $\mathfrak L_r\prpos t^{r-1}$ in all cases.

[Merge note. At $r=2$ this is Part~VII's two-piece ramp
(\cref{hc:sec:twopiece}): the deficit simplex is the interval of $2t\sqrt n+O(1)$
splits, the accepted mass is $3$, and $\varkappa_2\,2^3\cdot2t=12t/\sqrt\pi$ is
\eqref{hc:eq:ramp}; \eqref{gc:eq:shrinkingtail} at $r=2$ is the tail bound
\cref{hc:lem:pairtail}.]

\section{The background and microscopic terminal children}
\label{gc:sec:background}
With $r+1$ genuine pieces, both terminal children large, fixed words give the
Riemann integral on
\[
 \mathcal D_{r+1}(1/r)=\Bigl\{x_i>0:\ \sum x_i=1,\ x_i\le1/r\Bigr\}.
\]
Every pair sum exceeds $1/r$ except on cap-boundary faces. Delete thin boundary
strips first; the endpoint-prefix count then gives accepted mass
$2^{r-1}(r+2)$ and contribution $\mathfrak K_r$ after scaling by $n^{r/2}$. At
a fixed macroscopic cutoff, word-cost tails are removed by
\eqref{gc:eq:moments}.

The only singular integral corners have one coordinate tending to zero. Two
vanishing coordinates would leave at most $r-1$ cap-sized pieces, which cannot
sum to one. If $x_1=x$ is small, the remaining $r$ coordinates have deficits
from $1/r$ summing to $x$. Their cross-sectional volume is $O(x^{r-1})$ and they
stay bounded away from zero. Therefore the corner mass is
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:corner}
 O\!\left(\int_0^\varepsilon x^{r-5/2}\,\dd x\right)
 =O(\varepsilon^{r-3/2}).
\end{equation}
This proves finiteness and justifies restoring the integral corners.

The discrete family with one microscopic terminal child needs a separate bound,
because the parser imposes \eqref{hc:eq:capinterval}. Let that child have size
$a\le\varepsilon n$, with $r$ genuine large pieces remaining. At fixed $a,s$,
the number of choices is at most
\[
 K_r(a+1)(\Delta_++a+s+1)^{r-2}.
\]
Choose $\varepsilon,c<1/(4r)$. For $s\le cn$, all $r$ large sizes are at least
$n/(3r)$ for large $n$. Their Catalan bound is therefore independent of
$\varepsilon$. The entire scaled contribution is at most
\begin{equation}\label{gc:eq:smallterminal}
 K n^{-r+3/2}\sum_{a\le\varepsilon n}(a+1)c_a
            \sum_{s\le cn}T^{(g)}_s(\Delta_++a+s+1)^{r-2}.
\end{equation}
For $r=2$, this is $O(\sqrt\varepsilon)$. For $r\ge3$, split the power into
the $\Delta$, $a$, and $s$ terms. Respectively they give
\[
 O_B\!\left(\sqrt\varepsilon\,
              n^{-(r-2)/[2(r-1)]}\right),\qquad
 O(\varepsilon^{r-3/2}),\qquad
 O(\sqrt\varepsilon\,n^{-1/2}).
\]
For the last bound use
$\sum_{s\le cn}s^{r-2}T^{(g)}_s=O(n^{r-5/2})$.
The terms $s>cn$ are negligible by \eqref{gc:eq:tilt}, summing the remaining
weights before dividing by $c_n$. This proves that the small-terminal family
vanishes as the cutoffs are removed, including the degenerate child sizes zero.

For clarity, the exhaustive classification at a fixed cutoff is as follows. If
both terminal children are large, fewer than $r$ pieces force linear word cost;
$r$ pieces give the shrinking simplex; $r+1$ give the background; and at least
$r+2$ vanish by \eqref{gc:eq:many}. If one terminal child is small, fewer than
$r$ genuine pieces again force linear word cost, $r$ are covered by
\eqref{gc:eq:smallterminal}, and at least $r+1$ vanish by
\eqref{gc:eq:manysmall}. Both terminal children cannot be small. Small outer
decorations already belong to the small-word gaps, so there is no extra
mesoscopic family to add or double-count.

The limit order is explicit: first fix $\varepsilon'$, then choose
$\varepsilon$ small enough for the required tilts and terminal bounds; at those
cutoffs let $n\to\infty$ and then remove the fixed word cutoff $H$. Finally let
$\varepsilon'\downarrow0$ with its corresponding $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. The
shrinking-word error is controlled by \eqref{gc:eq:shrinkingtail}; background
corners and small terminal children by
\eqref{gc:eq:corner}--\eqref{gc:eq:smallterminal}. This proves pointwise
\eqref{gc:eq:window}. Since $p_n(m)$ is nondecreasing in $m$ and the limiting
function is continuous, the finite-grid monotonicity argument makes
convergence locally uniform in $t$. Taking $t=0$ also proves the endpoint
assertion in \cref{gc:thm:phase}.

[Merge note. At $r=2$ the background is Part~VII's three-piece contribution
(\cref{hc:sec:threepiece}), its accepted mass $2^{r-1}(r+2)=8$ is
\eqref{hc:eq:8}, the corner bound \eqref{gc:eq:corner} is the
$O(x^{-1/2}\,\dd x)$ corner of \cref{hc:sec:remainders}, and
\eqref{gc:eq:smallterminal} is \eqref{hc:eq:corner}; the monotone finite-grid
argument is that of \cref{hc:sec:evaluation}.]

\section{The first two reciprocal constants}
\label{gc:sec:constants}
The known integral $I_3(1/2)=12\pi$ (\cref{hc:sec:evaluation}) gives
$\mathfrak K_2=6$ and $\mathfrak L_2=12/\sqrt\pi$. For the next threshold we
give an ordinary-integral evaluation.

For $a<s<2a$, pair convolution gives
\[
 \mathcal P_a(s)=\int_{s-a}^{a}\frac{\dd x}{[x(s-x)]^{3/2}}
       =\frac{4(2a-s)}{s^2\sqrt{a(s-a)}}.
\]
Taking $a=1/3$ and pairing the four coordinates,
\begin{align*}
 I_4(1/3)
 &=48\int_{1/3}^{2/3}
       \frac{\sqrt{(2/3-s)(s-1/3)}}{s^2(1-s)^2}\,\dd s\\
 &=1728\int_{-1}^1\frac{\sqrt{1-x^2}}{(9-x^2)^2}\,\dd x.
\end{align*}
For $\gamma>1$, the substitution $x=\sin u$ yields
\[
 \mathcal J(\gamma)=\int_{-1}^1\frac{\sqrt{1-x^2}}{\gamma-x^2}\,\dd x
     =\pi\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac1\gamma}\right).
\]
Differentiation under this bounded integral gives
$-\mathcal J'(9)=\pi/(108\sqrt2)$. Hence
\[
 I_4(1/3)=8\sqrt2\,\pi,\qquad
 \mathfrak K_3=\frac5{\sqrt{2\pi}},\qquad
 \mathfrak L_3=\frac{729\sqrt3}{4\pi}.
\]
\begin{corollary}[The one-third window]\label{gc:cor:third}
Locally uniformly for real $t$,
\[
 n^{3/2}p_n\!\left(\left\lfloor n/3+t n^{3/4}\right\rfloor\right)
 \longrightarrow\frac5{\sqrt{2\pi}}
               +\frac{729\sqrt3}{4\pi}\prpos t^2.
\]
\end{corollary}

[Merge note. Numerically $\mathfrak K_3=1.99471\ldots$ and
$\mathfrak L_3=100.4797\ldots$. The delivered SymPy check
(\cref{gc:sec:finite}) verifies the pair antiderivative, its endpoint value and
$-1728\,\mathcal J'(9)=8\sqrt2\,\pi$, taking $\mathcal J$ as an input; an
independent mpmath quadrature made for this write-up confirms $\mathcal J(9)$,
both integral reductions and $I_4(1/3)$ to $4\cdot10^{-14}$. The next constant,
$\mathfrak K_4=\varkappa_5I_5(1/4)$, is not evaluated in closed form; two quadratures by different nested pair
convolutions, made for this write-up, agree on $I_5(1/4)\approx35.6357$, so
$\mathfrak K_4\approx0.6770$ (a numerical value, not a certified
enclosure).]

\section{Conditioned geometry}
\label{gc:sec:geometry}
The proof also gives marked limits. The macroscopic vector is ordered as the
two terminal children followed by the large outer decorations from the core
outward; outer entries are their costs, divided by $n$. Statements about its
dimension are interpreted by first taking $n\to\infty$ at a fixed cutoff and
then letting the cutoff tend to zero. The probability of excluded microscopic
terminal cases vanishes by the proved bounds.

\begin{corollary}[Piece and channel law in a phase]\label{gc:cor:geometry}
Condition on $M_n\le\lfloor\theta n\rfloor$, in a fixed phase of
\cref{gc:thm:phase}, including its allowed upper endpoint. The ordered
macroscopic-size vector converges to the probability density
\[
 I_p(\theta)^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^p x_i^{-3/2}
 \quad\text{on }\mathcal D_p(\theta).
\]
Independently of this vector, the endpoint/gap channel is uniform over $p+1$
possibilities: the low terminal-right endpoint with no append in any gap, or
the high endpoint with append-containing prefix length $0,\ldots,p-1$. Given a
channel, the gap words are independent, and a decorated word $w$ of the
prescribed type has probability $4^{-|w|}/2$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Insert a bounded continuous test function of the scaled pieces into the
fixed-word Riemann sums. The same absolute bounds control its tails. Each
allowed endpoint channel has limiting mass $\tfrac12\,2^{p-1}$, independent of
the piece sizes, giving the uniform channel law after \eqref{gc:eq:accepted}.
Fixed gap words contribute only their factors $4^{-|w|}$; the tail bound makes
their total cost tight. Dividing by the proved probability equivalent gives the
result, including independence.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The reciprocal-window mixture]\label{gc:cor:mixture}
Condition on the event in \eqref{gc:eq:window}. For fixed $t>0$, the $r$-piece
family has limiting weight
\[
 \frac{\mathfrak L_rt^{r-1}}{\mathfrak K_r+\mathfrak L_rt^{r-1}},
\]
and all its scaled sizes tend to $1/r$. Its cap deficits, divided by
$\Delta=rm-n$, converge to the uniform simplex law
$\operatorname{Dirichlet}(1,\ldots,1)$ on $r$ coordinates. The complementary
$(r+1)$-piece family has the density from \cref{gc:cor:geometry} at
$\theta=1/r$. For fixed $t\le0$, only the latter family survives.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The unconditioned family masses were proved separately. In the shrinking
family, fixed words give deficits summing to $\Delta+s+1$, with $s$ fixed under
truncation and then tight. Since $\Delta\to\infty$ for fixed $t>0$, this shift
disappears after normalization. The Catalan density is asymptotically constant
on the deficit simplex, so marked lattice sums give the uniform Dirichlet law.
The existing tail estimates justify removal of the word cutoff and division by
the total limiting mass. The background marked limit is the preceding Riemann
argument.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Merge note: the case $p=2$ is Part~IV's conditional law]
\label{gc:rem:geometry}
At $p=2$ and $1/2<\theta<1$, put $u=1-\theta$. The density of
\cref{gc:cor:geometry} is $I_2(\theta)^{-1}[x(1-x)]^{-3/2}$ on $[u,1-u]$, the
split law $f_u$ of Part~IV's \eqref{mj:eq:split-law} (with $I(u)=I_2(\theta)$
by \eqref{mj:eq:Iu}). The three uniform channels give an $A$-free word
probability $\tfrac13\cdot4^{-|w|}/2+\tfrac13\cdot4^{-|w|}/2=4^{-|w|}/3$ and a
word with an $A$ probability $\tfrac13\cdot4^{-|w|}/2=4^{-|w|}/6$, which is
Part~IV's universal word law \eqref{mj:eq:word-law}, and independence of word
and split is Part~IV's \cref{mj:thm:conditional} (manuscript~11 of batch~52).
The channel itself refines that theorem; it is the three equally weighted
endpoint channels of Part~IV's \cref{mj:cor:conditional} (manuscript~10 of
batch~52), there a sampling description and here part of a proved joint limit
for the parser's data. At $p=3$, $1/3<\theta\le1/2$, the corollary is the
conditioned three-piece law that Part~VII asked for (\cref{hc:sec:q-geometry}).
The merge checked the case $p=2$ against Part~IV's statements.
\end{remark}

independently generated avoiders through size eight, and exact finite
endpoint-prefix checks of \eqref{gc:eq:accepted}. Exact integer counts at the
one-third threshold give the rounded scaled probabilities of
\cref{gc:tab:finite}. At $n=192$ the value is $3.2587832927$, while the limit
is $5/\sqrt{2\pi}\approx1.9947114020$. These are slow-convergence regression
values, not error certificates or evidence replacing any proof step. Exact
count numerators and Catalan denominators are retained in the package.

\subsection{Attribution and repository boundary}
\label{gc:sec:boundary}
The public fixed-bound work of Nadler~\cite{Nadler} and
Mayama--Akita~\cite{MayamaAkita} provides the enumeration context. The
fewest-big-jumps principle~\cite{pr:KerriouMorters} is related heavy-tail
context, not an invoked theorem: the present parser and endpoint constraints
are handled directly. The source pin is the current combined article
containing Parts~VI and~VII, not its earlier incomplete merge. No external
paper is redistributed.

\subsection{Limits}
\label{gc:sec:limits}
The manuscript gives fixed-index equivalents, locally uniform fixed-index
crossover limits, and the stated conditioned laws. It does not give an
effective convergence rate, uniformity as $p$ or $r$ grows, or a bridge to the
fixed-bound spectral regime. It is an ordinary mathematical proof, not formally
verified or externally refereed, and makes no worldwide priority claim.

\section{Further research questions}
\label{gc:sec:research}
The manuscript states that it does not give an effective convergence rate,
uniformity as $p$ or $r$ grows, or a bridge to the fixed-bound spectral regime
(\cref{gc:sec:limits}). The subsections below turn that sentence into
questions; the pointers in parentheses are the merge's.

\subsection{Effective rates}
\label{gc:sec:q-rate}
Give a rate in \cref{gc:thm:phase,gc:thm:window}, and a uniform relative error
near every reciprocal boundary that overlaps the window scale. The one-third
table (\cref{gc:tab:finite}) converges slowly, and the proof's error terms at
$t=0$ are of order $n^{-1/2}$ only up to cutoff terms. (Part~VII's
\cref{hc:sec:q-errors} at $q=2$; Part~VI's \cref{pr:sec:q-uniform}.)

\subsection{Uniformity in the index}
\label{gc:sec:q-index}
Make the constants of \cref{gc:thm:phase,gc:thm:window} explicit in $p$ and $r$,
and find the range of growing indices on which the few-pieces description
survives. The endpoint-prefix mass $2^{p-2}(p+1)$ and the factor $\varkappa_p$
are explicit, but the cutoffs $\varepsilon$, $\varepsilon'$ and the tilt
depend on the index. (Part~VI's \cref{pr:sec:q-growing}.)

\subsection{The bridge to the fixed-bound spectral regime}
\label{gc:sec:q-spectral}
Connect the fixed-fraction theorems with the fixed-$m$ growth constants of
Parts~I and~II as $m/n\to0$. (Part~VI's \cref{pr:sec:q-growing}, Part~IV's
\cref{pr:sec:mj-bridge}, Part~III's \cref{lj:sec:q-reconnect}.)

\subsection{The whole conditioned permutation}
\label{gc:sec:q-whole}
\Cref{gc:cor:geometry} describes the parser's macroscopic pieces, channels and
gap words. Describe the positions and value sets of the macroscopic blocks in
the permutation and the location of the largest jump, as Part~VI's
\cref{pr:sec:q-geometry} and Part~IV's \cref{cjm:sec:mj-geometric} ask. (This
question is the merge's, from the part of Part~VI's question that the
manuscript leaves unanswered.)

\subsection{Closed forms of the constants}
\label{gc:sec:q-closed}
Evaluate $I_p(\theta)$ and $\mathfrak K_r$ in closed form beyond $p\le4$ and
$r\le3$, or show that the pair-convolution method stops there. (The merge's
question: the manuscript evaluates only $I_3(1/2)$ and $I_4(1/3)$.)

\phantomsection\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Appendices of Part I}
\appendix
\section{Exact small-bound algebra}
\label{app:algebra}
For $m=3$, the certified generating function is $A_3(x)=P_3(x)/Q_3(x)$, where
\begin{align*}
 P_3(x)={}&1-x-x^2+x^3+4x^4-4x^6-3x^7-3x^8\\
          &-5x^9-3x^{10}+2x^{11}+2x^{12},\\
 Q_3(x)={}&(x-1)(x+1)
 (x^5-x^4-x^3+x^2-2x+1)\\
          &\hspace{12mm}\cdot(x^6+x^5+x^4+2x^3+x^2-1).
\end{align*}
For $m=4$, the denominator factors as
\begin{align*}
 Q_4(x)={}&(x-1)\,D_U(x)\,D_V(x),\\
 D_U(x)={}&x^{10}+x^8+x^7-6x^5-x^4-x^3-x^2-x+1,\\
 D_V(x)={}&x^{12}-x^{11}-x^{10}-x^9+5x^8+4x^7+4x^6\\
          &+4x^5+5x^4+3x^3+x^2-1.
\end{align*}
Both displayed full denominators have constant term one. The numerator
of $A_4$, listed from constant term upwards, has coefficient vector
\[
\begin{gathered}
(1,-1,-1,-1,2,9,5,-3,-14,-10,-13,-23,\\
 -31,-11,21,32,21,1,-3,-5,-3,-3).
\end{gathered}
\]
It has degree 21 and $Q_4$ has degree 23. Thus the usual coefficient
recurrence from $Q_4A_4=P_4$ is valid for all $n\ge23$ when written using
23 preceding terms. The complete ascending coefficient arrays, full state
certificates, and exact checks are in the JSON files.

The primary reproducibility command needs no third-party Python packages:
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/verify.py
\end{lstlisting}
To regenerate numerical and symbolic proposals, install the packages in
\texttt{requirements.txt} and run
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/compute.py --max-m 20 --symbolic-max 4 --large-scalars
python code/verify.py
python code/plot_results.py
\end{lstlisting}
The generator deliberately raises an exception if it cannot convert a
numerical proposal to a valid exact certificate. A floating-point result
alone is never accepted as a proof of a spectral inequality.

To build the report from the supplied figures:
\begin{lstlisting}
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
The third pass settles page references and the table of contents if needed.
The TeX source uses standard packages from a current full TeX Live
installation; no font files are distributed.

The exact recurrence uses $(m+1)^2$ endpoint states and at most $m+1$
terms per state. Computing all coefficients through length $N$ therefore
uses $O(Nm^3)$ integer multiply-add operations, after coefficient
precomputation, and $O(Nm^2)$ stored integers in the supplied straightforward
implementation. These are arithmetic-operation counts, not bit-complexity
bounds. Exact values have growing bit length. A rolling buffer can reduce
storage when the complete coefficient table is not needed.

Perron certificates store integers $p,D$ for each radius endpoint $p/D$,
and a positive integer vector in the state order generated by the verifier.
Symbolic certificates store all polynomial coefficients in ascending degree.
The constant term of each common denominator is checked to be one.
The certificate identities prove infinitely many coefficients because the
formal system has a unique solution; the finite coefficient checks are
additional consistency tests.

\section{Source status and proof audit}
\label{app:status}
The literature check was performed on September 19, 2026. The inspected
primary items are the arXiv v1 versions listed below, including Nadler's
Conjecture~2 and Mayama--Akita's Theorem~4.14 and Problem~P3. The latter
paper's displayed numerical table and statement that its component comparison
was unproved were also inspected in the PDF. Targeted searches used the
paper titles and identifiers together with strict monotonicity, growth
constants, and asymptotic refinements. No later solution was located in
that search. This establishes the specific source-based status used here;
it is not an assertion that every possible publication has been searched.

The logical dependencies of the new claims are particularly short.
The strict-growth theorem uses the exact endpoint recurrence, the two
irreducible components, their root characterization, the proper-principal
Perron lemma, and the explicit shift of thresholds. This report includes
proofs of those prerequisites. The asymptotic theorem instead uses the
maximum-decomposition majorant, the injective skew-block construction,
the first-entry Catalan formula, and the truncated-tail lemma. In particular,
no numerical fit, unproved component-ordering pattern, or formal passage
from finite checks to all $m$ is used.

The work has not been formalized in a proof assistant. The integer and
polynomial certificates are executable verification of the stated finite
algebraic claims; they should not be described as a formal verification
of the analytic limiting argument or of the all-$m$ theorem.
